#6998. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

#6992. Silver Eye Makeup: “Stardust” Makeup Is Here to Bring Whimsy to Your Holiday Glam

No matter your feelings on how festive is too festive during the holiday season (for the record, we’re pro decorating your home like a European Christmas market from November to February), there’s one universal rule when it comes to beauty looks during this time that we can all agree on: The holidays were meant for glitter. And this year, we’re leaning into a more whimsical take on looking all shiny and bright. Say hello to “stardust” makeup.

This is more than just putting on a glitter eyeshadow or some tinsel in your hair. “Stardust” evokes a warm, nostalgic feeling and sprinkles some magic along the way. “Adding a pop of shimmer is such an act of self-celebration, and it feels really good,” says Donni Davy, celebrity makeup artist and founder of Half Magic. “It’s like decorating your face the same way you buy something beautiful for your home. It’s a feel-good thing that also sparks conversation and joy in other people and inspires other people to do the same.”

“[It]’s something magical that gives us the same feeling we had as kids opening presents,” adds celebrity makeup artist Alexandra French. “I’m always letting my inner child play when creating these looks. I’ll never not be whimsical."

The most obvious way to add some sparkle is with the eyes. But for that “stardust” effect, there’s a delicate art to it. “Stardust eyes are all about creating that dreamy, ethereal twinkling effect,” says Davy. “It's not about packing on a ton of glitter; it’s more about these delicate flashes of sparkle that catch in the light in a really magical way.”

The key to the “stardust” effect is in the application. Compared to traditional glitter or gems, Davy says that stardust eyes are more subtle. “It’s almost like a dusting that catches the light in a totally unexpected and very flattering way.”

For Davy, she loves starting with a soft wash of color (pale pink, lavender, or champagne are her personal picks) on the lids, like Half Magic’s Duo-Chrome Eyeshadow in Blushing Lizard. You can also try Saie’s viral Supersuede Baked Eyeshadow in Bellini or K-beauty’s Hince Chill Wave for the same shimmer effect. Apply shadow with your fingers, she instructs, focusing on the center of the lid and then blending out. “You want the sparkle to look almost random, rather than perfectly placed,” she says. “If you want a bit of a grungier look, you can use a brush to apply some underneath your eye as well.”

For a more ethereal look, build up the shine. She recommends adding some color to the inner and outer waterline corners for added dimension and again all over your eyelids. “You’re going to feel like yourself, but just sparkly,” Davy says.

But for those who want to go full glam, pack it all on. Think Alix Earle’s Halloween costume as a “gynoid.” Inspired by Japanese illustrator and artist Hajime Sorayama’s “sexy robot” illustrations, French leaned all the way in with silver.

“I always said for silver eye makeup to look truly metallic you need to layer different levels of textures and finish it off with a high-shine sparkle,” French says. “I wanted the eyes to feel like this mask that sparkles and looks alive the way his paintings feel so alive. I wanted people to stop in their tracks when they saw it from across the room.”

Here, she layered the Urban Decay Chrome Melt Eye Shadow in Temper Tantrum on top of the MAC Dazzleshadow Liquid Eyeshadow for that bold metallic eye. Then she topped it off with Question Beauty Silver Mirror Glitter (her pro tip: Use eyelash glue for application with this) to elevate the metallic look and get something with a more reflective sheen.

But no matter how intense you go with your eye look, both Davy and French say you can never go wrong with sparkle. “It’s a simple way to add a little something to your everyday makeup,” says French.

Why stop at just the eyes? This holiday season, experts say to sprinkle some “stardust” all over. For the body, Davy likes shimmer on the collarbones, shoulders, and décolletage. “Especially if you’re wearing something that shows a little skin,” she says. Try Half Magic’s Glitterpuck, which she says is a water-based formula made for zero glitter fallout, or we love the Marc Jacobs Money Shot Highlighter and Eva NYC’s Lightweight Silver Hair & Body Glitter Shimmer Spray for an iridescent shine. French recommends using the One/Size On ’Til Dawn Mattifying Waterproof Setting Spray mixed with glitter. She also recommends using the Fenty Beauty Diamond Bomb All-Over Diamond Veil for some body sparkle and more. “It’s the best,” she says. “I use it in the hair as well.”

And everyone’s nails have been getting the stardust treatment as early as October, with Taylor Swift wearing a faint, gold “fairy dust”shimmer on her nails at the 2026 Academy Museum Gala. If shadow or body glitter doesn’t speak to you, stardust nails might be the perfect fit.

“I love depth and dimension, and that’s exactly what this is,” says celebrity nail artist Tom Bachik. “The way this differs from just applying a cat eye or a chrome powder is by actually adding them all. It’s all about layering to get that Stardust effect. You want to have a nice shimmer and iridescence, and chunky glitter, and even a few rhinestones thrown in for good measure.”

Inspired by Christmas, Bachik wanted to play off gold with a pink iridescent shimmer for Selena Gomez’s latest mani. It’s a subtle pop of color that gives that golden sparkle some added depth. He used the Apreś Nails Maisie Neutral Coffin Gelx Tips before painting on BS-101 and Cosmic Chrome in Etherea from his own collection. Then he used a 3-D gel for that raised gold detail for a nice added touch, and to capture all that holiday spirit. “People love a little sparkle and this is the time of year that actually gives you permission to wear it,” he says.

So go get your whimsy on this holiday season. It’ll make this time just a little bit more special.

#6990. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

#6987. Gloria Steinem’s Memorial Was a Celebration—and One Big Talking Circle

“Celebrating the glorious life of Gloria.” Those were the words projected against a red backdrop behind the stage at the Park Avenue Armory on Wednesday evening. Politicians (some, like Minnesota’s Peggy Flanagan, newly elected in the midterm Democratic sweep), performers, and poets gathered alongside Gloria Steinem’s family and chosen family to memorialize the pioneering activist, who died in September at 92 years old.

The mood was celebratory, which is, according to her friends who organized the occasion, exactly what she wanted. That, and no mentions of God, said fellow activist and author Amy Richards, drawing a laugh from the crowd.

Former first ladies Michelle Obama and Jill Biden were among those front and center, with Meg Ryan, Marisa Tomei, Bette Midler, Diane von Furstenberg, Ashley Judd, Thelma Golden, Lucy Liu, Keri Russell, Candice Bergen, Annie Leibovitz, Anita Hill, Christy Turlington, and Padma Lakshmi. Attendees sat in chairs arranged in giant semi-circles to mirror the talking circles Steinem regularly hosted at her Upper East Side apartment (the final one took place with a group of teenagers just three weeks before her death). The guest list reflected Steinem’s wide-ranging reach. “There’s nothing intimidating about this room at all,” quipped producer and philanthropist Abigail Disney.

The night kicked off with the song “Hold On” by Heidi Wilson, which was written in 2018 after the suicide of a Vermont teenager and went on to be adopted as an anthem of solidarity during more recent protests in Minneapolis and New York. It was performed by a group dubbed ‘Gloria’s Choir,’ which included the Indigo Girls, actress Emma Watson, Leona Naess, and the members of indie rock band Betty, who have been together since 1986. “Gloria was our favorite rockstar,” said Betty bassist Alyson Palmer, beaming.

Alice Walker, one of Steinem’s closest friends, couldn’t be there, so Mariska Hargitay took to the stage to read “She,” a poem Walker wrote for Steinem on her 75th birthday: “She is the one whowithout hesitation comes to my aid and my defense. / She is the one who believes my side of the story first. / She is the one whose heart is open. / She is our sister, our teacher, our friend.”

Barbara Lee, the first Black woman mayor of Oakland and Steinem’s friend and collaborator of over 50 years, spoke passionately about their early activist years with Bella Abzug and campaigning for Shirley Chisholm. “When others stepped away, Gloria stepped forward,” she said.

“Hello everybody, I’m Marlo,” said a glowing Marlo Thomas as she adjusted the mic, as if anyone needed an introduction. It was during her time playing the single, struggling actress Ann Marie on That Girl that she first crossed paths with Steinem. Thomas was approached about playing Steinem on screen and in a meeting for the project the would-be producer looked at the two of them and said, “I don’t know which one of you I want to f*ck first.” The meeting was a bust, of course, Thomas said, but their friendship endured and the two spent countless holidays together. Thomas was at Steinem’s bedside during her final days, reading Native American prayers and Googling the Kaddish so she could recite it to her.

Musician Blake Morgan read a poem by his mother Robin Morgan, the poet, activist, and editor of the seminal feminist anthology Sisterhood Is Powerful, who died a few days after Steinem. Morgan and Steinem, along with three other women, had met monthly for dinner for the past 50 years, and their enduring friendship inspired the words of the poem “Giants at the Corner Table.” “There will be three someday, then two, then one. / That one will carry in herself some of the best of all of us because in each other’s presence some of the best of each of us came forth. / And when she goes, the women we were to one another will all wink out, the work we’ve shared lasting perhaps a little while beyond us.”

Cyndi Lauper, in leather pants and pink-streaked hair, declared that “without Gloria, there wouldn’t have been ‘Girls Just Wanna Have Fun,’” before launching into an acoustic rendition of her most famous song. Obama was the first one in the crowd to stand up and start dancing and singing along, prompting everyone to follow her lead.

Chelsea Handler brought laughs—even without ribbing men—and Amanda Gorman gave a captivating recitation of her poem “We Rise.” Cynthia Erivo was initially slated to sing “I Won’t Complain,” but changed course after hearing the speeches to perform “Imagine” instead. “Gloria imagined possibility,” she said, before giving an electrifying rendition of the John Lennon classic that reflected the night’s message of hope: every attendee was asked to wear a button that said “Hope-aholic.”

Public-interest lawyer, social justice activist, and founder of the Equal Justice Initiative Bryan Stevenson delivered what might have been the night’s most impassioned tribute to Steinem, using anecdotes from his own upbringing and their longstanding friendship to highlight her feminist and anti-racism stance. “Is there a woman who made you?” he recalled Steinem asking him after he shared a story with her about when he first declared himself a feminist. His grandmother, he said—the daughter of people enslaved in Virginia and his family’s lodestar, who would also live into her nineties, and whom he could still feel hugging him in moments of conflict and challenge. Stevenson said Steinem, like his grandmother, always moved with love, ending with a powerful message that left the audience in tears: “To be free is to do what’s right, and Gloria died a free woman, which is rare, because of how she lived her life.”

Former senator Hillary Rodham Clinton, with her daughter Chelsea looking on, gave a closing speech that highlighted Steinem’s love of Louisa May Alcott (an image of Little Women hung behind the stage along with other banners that read “Smash the Patriarchy” and “It’s Steinemite!”). Clinton spoke about her admiration for Steinem, which started when she was in college at the height of the women’s movement, and how Steinem’s work paved a path for her own political career. “She helped make it possible for someone like me to run for president, to win a major party nomination and the popular vote… in case you forgot,” Clinton said, smiling, bringing an uproarious crowd to its feet. Clinton held a torch aloft in her hands as she echoed Steinem’s mission of passing it on to others. “Gloria gave us 92 years of light,” Clinton said, “and all the tools, memories, inspiration, and hope that we need to keep that light burning.”

The celebration ended as an all-out party as Kathy Sledge took to the stage, backed by Gloria’s Choir and Lauper, to belt out “We Are Family.” A solemn memorial service, this was not. The audience sang, clapped, danced, and joyfully waved their hands in unison. Fogo Azul, a New York-based reggae samba drum line of women, transgender, and nonbinary percussionists, appeared to ecstatically escort the crowd out of the massive room. It turns out that the timing of this week’s event was fortuitous: It fell on what would have been the birthday of Steinem’s friend, the late Native American activist Wilma Mankiller (who also wrote the lyrics for one of the night’s musical tributes, an art song for voice and string quartet in the Cherokee language by composer Anjelica Lindsey called “I Want to Be Reincarnated as Gloria Steinem”) and days before a memorial honoring labor leader Dolores Huerta.

Almost everyone who took the stage spoke about the importance not just of preserving Steinem’s memory, but of continuing her work; of carrying on her activism and organizing; of not retreating from her commitment to equality and justice; of why it was necessary to “keep on keeping on,” as Bryan Stevenson phrased it.

“We will not let her work end without her physical presence,” Barbara Lee said. Gloria’s Foundation is already proof of that. The organization continues to raise money to carry on her mission and even preserve her apartment as a refuge for women’s activism and gathering in perpetuity. Steinem may be gone, but this week made clear that her spirit is still very much alive and in capable hands. As Mariska Hargitay said from the stage, “Gloria will always exist in the present tense.”

Jill Biden, Amanda Gorman, Bryan Stevenson

Tiffany Shlain, A Dendrobiography of Gloria Steinem, 2026

Hillary Clinton, Jill Biden, Michelle Obama

#6984. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

#6982. The Chanel Rain Boots Are the New Controversial Shoe of the Season

This fall, fashion has been in the thrall of a freaky shoe—the freakier, the better. Designers have been putting forward unorthodox footwear, and people—especially celebrities—have been going totally mad for them. Remember just earlier this year when Alexander Skarsgård rocked clear-PVC, toe-exposing Saint Laurent dress shoes? Or when Margaret Qualley donned Chanel’s “barefoot sandals,” which are merely a luxurious leather cap for your heels? The mood of the season is all about slipping into a pair of shoes that instantly make everyone around do a double take.

And now, as we near the end of a year rich in unhinged accessories, a new freaky shoe contender has entered the ring. . . courtesy, once again, of Chanel’s Mathieu Blazy. Here’s a hint of the unexpected breakout hit: You can wear them rain or shine, but they’re especially useful in a downpour—and even more so in a full-on flood.

And so, meet Blazy’s thigh-high take on the classic rain boot. Back in April, the designer unveiled his resort 2027 collection in Biarritz, and what came stomping down the runway were two pairs of the tall wader-style rubber boots. Chanel has long designed Wellington boots, of course—but these felt especially fresh and unexpected, thanks to their elongated silhouette and bright, primary-colored trims.

They were a perfectly thematic shoe offering for the show, given Blazy was inspired by the beachy town of Biarritz, which put him in a decidedly nautical mood. The thigh-highs, then, evoked the very same boots that many fishermen wear while on the job searching for their catch of the day. But if you think that the dramatic, slightly-cartoony shoes were a gimmick relegated solely to the runway, well, think again. In the same way that people are flocking to the Chanel stores to secure their barefoot sandals, many have been snapping up the rain boots this month, the very moment they hit the shelves.

First things first: the polarizing rain boots retail for $2,800 and are made of caoutchouc—a natural rubber. They come in black or white, and despite their extreme silhouette, shoppers have already been enthusiastically posting their outfits of the day, with the boots as the pièce de résistance. On TikTok, personal shopper and buyer Marci Hirshleifer Penn tried on the white versuib, and went instantly viral. “Channel 4 News said there’s a light drizzle today,” she jokes, adding, “Don’t think twice about them—they’re a total collector's piece.”

TikTok creator @Yofancyyy tried on the same colorway, adding, “Y’all ain’t got these”—which divided the comments. (One user comment: “And I don’t want them.”) Influencer Bryanboy even took the black version into a lake, in an attempt to see if they can withstand hefty water conditions. “I’m like The Little Mermaid,” he says, wading into the sea and perching on top of a rock. “A little Chanel Mermaid.”

It may seem surprising that so many are willing to embrace what is essentially a pantaboot, but Penn said it best: They are to be treated as more of a collector’s item than a day-to-day shoe. Blazy’s more-wearable pumps have already been a commercial best-seller, but it’s impressive that his more daring runway styles—from the barefoot sandals to, now, the rain boots—are proving just as desirable. Clearly, kitschy luxury is proving to be just as investment-worthy as, say, a timeless cashmere crewneck from Loro Piana or The Row.

The question is: Would you spend rent on a pair of thigh-high rain boots? Scott not: In ten years from now, they could be worth double. And at least you’d have something to wear for the next nor’easter. After all, finding non-hideous climate-ready boots is hard.

#6979. THE Journal Reveals 2026 Product of the Year Winners

Sixteen companies were selected as winners for their product achievements.

THE Journal has announced the winners of its 2026 Product of the Year Awards. Since 2020, these awards have recognized outstanding product development achievements of manufacturers and suppliers whose products or services are considered to be particularly noteworthy in the transformation of education technology.

This year, 16 companies were recognized across multiple categories for their impact in education. For the full list of winners, visit THEJournal.com.

"Congratulations to all of our 2026 Product of the Year Award winners," said Rhea Kelly, editor-in-chief of THE Journal. "These innovative solutions reflect the continued evolution of education technology and the important role it plays in supporting educators, improving student experiences and helping institutions navigate an increasingly digital future. We're proud to recognize the companies driving meaningful progress across education institutions."

#6973. Testing, Testing! The Vogue Beauty Board Puts Fall’s Buzziest Beauty Products to the Test

Testing, testing—beauty product testing, that is! A quick trip to the beauty closet would reveal that deliveries are never scarce—with our mail room frequently depositing everything from forthcoming launches to restocks of longstanding favorites to the shelves of Vogue’s beauty team. Dare go on a holiday, and editors will find a mountain of deliveries waiting to be unboxed when they return (enough to earn a spot on the reality TV show Hoarders, or, if you’re like me, trigger your eco-anxiety). Yes, it’s a champagne problem to have, which is why we are sharing the wealth with our fellow Vogue Beauty Board members to broaden the scope of perspective.

So, what to test, and where to even begin? There’s skincare, makeup, haircare (and more)—oh my! Everyone’s got favorite categories—sometimes our product picks are driven by preference. Take Vogue’s beauty shopping editor, Kiana Murden who will never say no to a lip balm; or perhaps necessity sparks the insatiable urge to discover—I have found a new favorite finishing oil to add shine to my just-blown out hair.

As for our newly-established Beauty Board—our community of experts, founders, and board-certified practitioners—they will test a carefully curated batch of products and share their candid take. Below a look at what we tested. Check back each month to see what we’ve been trying.

About the Product: Dior’s skin tint is a triple-threat—complexion coverage, SPF protection, and a skincare-infused formula. To start, it comes in 24 shades, all offering light to medium coverage. Ingredients like fermented collagen, glycerin, and vitamin E hydrate skin for seamless application, while hybrid sunscreen ingredients offer protection up to SPF 20.

About the Product: Yep, Hermès does beauty too—and their lip mask leaves your pout soft and supple. Made in Italy with 97% naturally-derived ingredients, Hermes’s lip mask repairs chapped lips with hyaluronic acid, sesame seed extract, ceramides, and astaxanthin (that’s where that orange hue comes from).

About the Product: CurrentBody’s latest LED mask innovation elevates the brand’s existing library of tech. Series 3 has had a major boost in bulbs from 236 to 750, designed to treat everything from hyperpigmentation to fine lines. Board-certified nurse practitioner Natia Rufolo particularly likes its customization features: “It incorporates six wavelengths, including 633-nanometer red, 830-nanometer near-infrared, and 1072-nanometer deep near-infrared, alongside blue, green, and yellow light, so it goes beyond a traditional red-and-near-infrared mask,” she said in our best red light therapy guide.

About the Product: Jones Road’s brightening concealer uses a trio of botanical extracts (marshmallow root, rice bran, and licorice root) to help brighten discoloration while offering buildable medium coverage. Meanwhile, shea butter allows the dense, creamy formula to sink into the skin for an entirely natural, non-cakey finish—and for even more coverage, this pick pairs well with the brand’s neutralizer.

About the Product: A follow up to TIRTIR’s viral semi-matte cushion foundation, its Tinted Skin Glow Cushion offers a luminous alternative. Here, the formula features 73% skin-care ingredients and a serum-like texture that just bounces into the complexion. A blend of niacinamide, peptides, hyaluronic acid, and botanical oils deliver hydrating, plumping benefits while offering medium, buildable coverage.

About the Product: La Mer offers some of the most prestigious skincare on the market—in both price and formulation. Aside from the brand’s signature concentrated Miracle Broth (a blend of fermented kelp extract, vitamins, and nutrients), The Concentrate brings together lime tea concentrate and botanic oils to soothe irritated skin—whether you’re combating general redness or post-procedural skin.

About the Product: Christophe Robin’s lightweight dry oil does a bit of everything—hydrates, adds shine, and keeps frizz in check. Use it before styling for heat protection, or after to boost shine and smooth flyaways. The formula combines amla, coconut, tung wood, and oat oils, plus skincare-quality ingredients like squalane and ceramides, to leave your hair feeling soft and healthy.

About the Product: Part skin tint, part mineral sunscreen, Carte Blanche’s Multi-Hyphenate offers sheer complexion coverage with a barely-there feel. The star ingredient, exosomes derived from licorice and flax plant, helps brighten hyperpigmentation, while vitamin C and bakuchiol offer added antioxidant support. Plus, squalane and panthenol soothe and smooth dehydrated skin.

About the Product: Stubborn breakouts and irritated complexions are no match for Serum Vivant. Inspired by Biologique Recherche’s best-selling Masque Vivant, this formula treats adult blemishes through a mix of prebiotics, postbiotics, and fermented extracts to support the skin’s microbiome, sans dryness or discomfort.

About the Product: SK-II lovers to the front! There’s a new serum to know. Introducing its Facial Treatment Serum—infused with “boosting oil pearls” rich with vitamin E and castor oil, bursting upon application to dispense the perfect amount of product without loosing a lightweight feel. Then, niacinamide and galactomyces ferment filtrate (GFF) are in the mix too for added brightening.

About the Product: 111Skin’s rinse-off peel exfoliates with a blend of AHAs, BHAs, PHAs, and botanical extracts. Papain (AKA papaya enzymes) and milk thistle extract help to slough off dead skin and brighten hyperpigmentation. Meanwhile, glutathione helps fight against oxidative stress.

About the Product: L’Oréal Paris offers an affordable moisturizer that balances active ingredients alongside hydrating humectants. The super-rich texture applies like butter, combining hyaluronic acid to boost water retention, retinol to smooth skin and target signs of aging, and vitamin C to brighten your complexion. The good news, though, is that it’s safe for sensitive skin, given retinol and vitamin C’s relatively low concentration.

When we test and review a product, we take a holistic approach to deliver well-rounded product recommendations. First, we lean on Vogue’s vast network of experts—from board-certified dermatologists to celebrity estheticians—to gain professional acumen on the industry’s standout products, ones these specialists would actually use on their clients. We pair their expertise with our editorial best practices to curate the thoughtful edits you read on our site.

We selected the best based on the following characteristics: ingredients, type, texture, packaging, finish, and the concerns they address. #6969. Fear of God Fall 2027 Ready-to-Wear

After spending almost a year with his design team in Paris, Jerry Lorenzo moved it back to Los Angeles, while continuing to develop Fear of God’s collections in Milan: “I just felt like—creatively, emotionally—I needed to be rooted in my city,” he said. The decision influenced TEN, his latest collection and the tenth numbered offering in thirteen years. “It doesn't have the cleanliness of Paris,” he acknowledged. The designer hadn't planned for the tenth collection to mark any particular milestone, but described its timing as “providential,” considering the spiritual significance of the number. Bringing together everything from tailoring to vintage-inspired sportswear, he left nothing out: Or, as he put it: “To say something that's unequivocally yours, you have to put all of you into it.”

There was no particular theme this season. Lorenzo cited everything from Keanu Reeves in flannel to Michael Jordan in a suit to Allen Iverson walking into a basketball game among the references that have long informed his work. Instead, the inspiration came from “knowing who we are,” he said. Having navigated what he described as a difficult year, he considers TEN one of his most emotional collections, shaped as much by setbacks as by successes. As a young brand, Fear of God had sometimes tried to “serve an industry,” rather than its own audience: “It's easy to get lost there,” Lorenzo said.

The tailoring was generous yet controlled: long black overcoats layered over grey waistcoats and wide trousers, cropped leather jackets paired with fluid suiting, and softly structured blazers with exaggerated shoulders. A belted black leather coat came with bright red gloves, while quilted leather jackets, faded denim, and washed workwear introduced more rugged textures. The palette moved between black, charcoal, olive, earthy browns and washed-out blue, punctuated by flashes of red. Alongside familiar wool-cashmere fabrics and American jersey, Lorenzo introduced velour, new leathers and “a lot of Japanese fabrics,” including denim and canvas. Bonded details and articulated sleeves added structure to the oversized silhouettes.

The American influence was particularly evident in Athletics, the category rooted in Lorenzo's personal approach to sportswear: “I don't ever put anything technical on,” he said of his gym wardrobe. The collection also introduces the Fear of God Trainer, the brand's first athletic shoe developed entirely in-house, with supercritical foam cushioning. Rather than relying on conspicuous branding, Lorenzo believes its identity is immediately recognizable through its design: “Shape and materials are undeniably ours.” After years of collaborations with sportswear partners, the experience brought less a lesson than a realization: “You kind of realize you could have done it on your own the whole time.”

#6964. On the Podcast: Anthony Boyle On Transforming Into Sam Bankman-Fried and His Best Bird Calls

On a crisp mid-November morning, Anthony Boyle arrived to the podcast studio wearing a leather jacket, or as our Head of Editorial Content, Chloe Malle, called it “Jensen’s jacket”—a reference to the signature leather jacket donned by disgraced Nvidia founder Jensen Huang. There has been much chatter in Vogue offices about the correspondence between his leather jacket uniform and the fact that he was having an affair. “He’s a top shagger,” Boyle joked, “it’s an homage to him.”

This coincidence felt topical given the big tech moment we are in as a culture, as well as the fact that Boyle came on the podcast to speak with Chloe and our Deputy Editor Taylor Antrim about his role as Sam Bankman-Fried in the new series The Altruists, which is out today on Netflix. Boyle stars alongside Julia Garner, who plays Bankman-Fried’s on-again, off-again girlfriend and business partner, Caroline Ellison. The two of them portray the nerdy genius couple that made it big during the cryptocurrency boom and then met their spectacular undoing after being caught stealing billions from customers.

Unlike many of his previous roles—which had him portraying mustachioed Irishmen in period garb—Boyle spoke about the source material he mined in service of his transformation into this awkward American mastermind. “There was a lot of footage of him. The lad loved giving interviews,” Boyle explained, “Even at the collapse [of the company], when all the lawyers were like, ‘Stop giving interviews,’ he was like, ‘Just one more…’”

Boyle once attended a Los Angeles warehouse party with some “crypto lads” as a form of character study. “I thought there was something really interesting in that no one had ever looked at these guys. These were guys who were in their mothers’ basement, and they suddenly became billionaires. Suddenly they had power, and they had money, which in America makes you a god. If you have money in Ireland, it makes you a wanker,” he laughed, “I do think he truly believed in effective altruism, but, you know, the best laid plans of mice and men gang aft agley.”

They also discussed several of his other roles from his Broadway debut in Harry Potter and the Cursed Child to his beloved performance in Say Nothing. He reminisced about his years living in midtown Manhattan, including a “great little Irish bar in Port Authority.” During his rare moments of downtime, he’s been known to watch reality television. “Anything with ‘love’ in the title, I’ll watch.”

#6963. The Property Brothers Reveal Why Lighting Tops Their Holiday Chore Checklist

The holidays are upon us, and while we busy our hands with turkeys, trees, and tinsel, the Property Brothers are gifting fans a few friendly tips for hosting across generations. Partnering with Home Instead, Drew and Jonathan Scott have developed a holiday chore checklist that every host should complete before guests arrive.

“The holidays are all about bringing family together. But when you're hosting aging loved ones, a little preparation can go a long way in making sure everyone feels comfortable and safe. As home renovation experts, we know that it's often the smallest changes that make the biggest difference,” the brothers explain.

Ahead, we’re sharing their top tips for a safe, festive, and inviting home this holiday season.

While string lights and extension cords are all holiday decor staples, they can be a major tripping hazard. To prevent spending the holidays in the emergency room, the brothers recommend double-checking that loose ends and loops are neatly tucked away to keep paths and walkways clear throughout your home.

Slippery rugs, mats, and runners are another major safety hazard. Drew and Jonathan recommend reinforcing any pieces that could potentially slide with gripping tabs or replacing them with options that have better traction—especially in bathrooms where wet floors pose a greater risk.

While decorating with Christmas lights is at the top of everyone’s to-do list, your home needs more than a well-lit tree. Dark halls and stairways are major safety concerns, especially when hosting guests unfamiliar with your floor plan. Plug-in nightlights are a great option for improved visibility, and they can be decorative too.

A clear entryway for the holidays is a nonnegotiable. Outside the home, leaves and debris lining your front steps are as dangerous as they are unsightly. Before your guests ring the doorbell, take the time to rake, sweep, and clean as much as you can to ensure all steps, dips, and curbs are visible.

Drew and Jonathan clock in at well over six feet tall, but their guests may not be as gifted with height. If you’re used to storing things up high, the brothers recommend moving essentials like plates and glasses from high shelves and into lower, more easily accessible territory. Not only does this minimize straining, but it also reduces the risk of dropping pieces that could leave dangerous, messy shards in their wake.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6961. Stars Joined Polo Ralph Lauren to Toast the New Blaze Bag at Sunset Tower

Though Polo by Ralph Lauren may conjure up images of days spent in the countryside, the brand celebrated the launch of its new Blaze bag in quintessential California fashion on Wednesday afternoon, with a poolside lunch at Sunset Tower Hotel.

On a refreshingly cool day, the likes of Chloe Bennet, Charlotte Lawrence, Laura Harrier, Diana Silvers, and Justine Lupe descended upon the storied hotel with the new purses slung over their shoulders.

Named after the white marking on a horse’s face, the Blaze, which launched as part of Fall 2026, has already become a staple in Lawrence’s rotation. “It’s been my number-one bag. I’ve been using it for three months now,” the “Sleep Talking” singer told Vogue. “It’s so cute, and it’s also way bigger than it looks. It’s even been my carry-on when I’m traveling.”

Though most attendees arrived with the aforementioned bag in tow, each put their own spin on Ralph Lauren’s signature Western-inspired preppy aesthetic. Fringe was a recurring theme, with both Bennet and singer Scout Willis in leather and suede jackets. Harrier, meanwhile, leaned into her crisp style in a white button-up and straight-leg jeans, while podcaster Liz Perez opted for the same pared-back formula.

Lupe, best known lately for her role in Nobody Wants This, eschewed outerwear in favor of a long-sleeve navy-and-white striped polo top and fiery red sweater draped over her shoulders. “I love something that feels boyish and androgynous, but also feminine at the same time,” she explained. “And I think a polo does a really great job of marrying those two qualities.”

For Lupe, who lives across town in Eagle Rock, any visit to the Sunset Tower Hotel feels like a special occasion. “I feel like I'm doing something fancy, and it’s never a casual thing. It’s like my equivalent of when you’re a kid and you’re dressing up to go out with your grandparents,” the actress shared.

For the first hour, the group socialized inside as servers strolled around with mini lobster rolls, watermelon and feta bites, and Champagne. When it came time for lunch, attendees were greeted by a long table adorned with burgundy floral arrangements. Before taking their seats, some stopped to snap photos of the picturesque white-and-maroon umbrellas overlooking the pool. A chopped salad with tomato, cucumber, fennel, chickpeas, haricots verts, and mozzarella kicked off lunch. For the main course, guests chose between chicken paillard and grilled salmon, both served atop a bed of arugula.

Willis and Lawrence settled in next to each other, facing Harrier and chef Eleonore Toulin. Farther down the table, writers Laurel Pantin, Amanda Alagem, and Alessandra Codinha caught up and swapped shopping recommendations. Sorbet with fresh berries gave guests an excuse to linger a little longer, soaking up the scenic views and mild afternoon air before heading back to their day.

Casey Fremont Crowe, Alessandra Codinha, Rebecca Ramsey

#6954. My Packing Style: Tastemaker TyLynn Graham

When it comes to mastering the art of the travel wardrobe, it’s no surprise that our favorite fashion insiders also moonlight as packing pros. In My Packing Style, we tap some of the most well-traveled people we know for a peek inside their travel bags and vacation wardrobes to see how they take their style on the road. You’ve bookmarked the trip, you’ve screenshotted the outfits; now, a look at how it all comes together.

For those on the East Coast, there are still a couple precious weeks of fall to take advantage of before winter hits. The apple orchards are pouring the last of the hot cider, and leaf-peeping season is at its tail-end. For TyLynn Graham, tastemaker and curator of Vette storefront TyLynn.shop, this means one thing: It’s time to hit the road for a cozy weekend in Upstate New York.

“For a road trip, my packing strategy is different because I don’t have to worry about luggage restrictions,” says Graham of her planning process—though she still plots her outfits ahead of time, the key, she says, to stress-free travel days getting ready in the morning. “I also keep a few essentials, like a scarf, my beauty bag, and an extra sweater, within reach in the car. There’s something really lovely about having the freedom to bring a little more of home with you.”

Her fall travel uniform is as cozy as you might expect. There’s flannel, of course, alongside other tactile materials like corduroy and wool; a palette of browns and oatmeal hues feels especially apropos for the destination. She booked in for a few days at Inness, located in the heart of the Hudson Valley. “It was the perfect place to spend a few days in November,” she adds.

Ahead, here’s everything Graham packed for a long fall weekend Upstate—along with her tips for creating easy outfit formulas on the road.

I start brainstorming and visualizing what I want to wear about a week before a trip, but I pack the night before. Everything stays in my closet until then—I write down my looks in a notebook, so the packing starts on paper.

After everything is in the notebook, I do pack in outfits. I gravitate toward natural tones—black, white, cream, and navy—with good patterns and plaids mixed in. That palette helps everything feel cohesive while still leaving me room to play. Because this was a road trip, I had the luxury of bringing a few extra things; I still planned my looks, but found room for another sweater or jacket.

For this trip, I gravitated toward Guest in Residence, Agolde, and Jeanerica. Their knits, denim, and easy layers felt right for a few autumn days in the countryside.

For this road trip, I brought my REI Co-op Trailgate Gear Bin. It fits everything! I love being able to bring the extra layers and familiar things I want for a few days away. I also brought my one-of-a-kind Bode equestrian bag—the equestrian references fit the feeling of this trip beautifully.

Jeans, a tee, a great sweater, and good boots. Simple and comfortable, but still put together. For this trip, I brought my Blundstones—perfect with denim, corduroy, and all the cozy layers.

My Louis Vuitton makeup bag always comes with me, whether I’m flying or driving. It keeps my beauty essentials together and brings a little of my home routine with me wherever I’m staying.

Both, actually. I love a good sweater and a scarf—I’m always happy to have an extra layer. For this trip, I packed my Toteme scarf along with plenty of knitwear.

Lesse face spray—it’s a staple in my travel beauty bag.

I keep accessories pretty minimal. My Tiffany & Co. watch is my go-to for every day, so it comes with me everywhere. I also brought a black leather belt with silver hardware that I know works well with both jeans and corduroy pants.

No—I gravitate toward fabrics that need very little maintenance. I like being able to unpack, get dressed, and enjoy wherever I am without too much fuss.

My Blundstone boots! They’re the pair I reach for for a few days in the countryside. I love them with corduroy, a plaid shirt, and an oversized sweater.

My Toteme scarf. I wear it around my head or neck, either draped open or tied in a knot. It’s really beautiful, and changing how I wear it gives the same outfit a different feeling. I love it with a simple tee and sweater or tucked into a jacket when it’s particularly cold.

I like to leave a little room for things I find along the way—plus, a road trip gives me a bit more freedom to bring something home.

Bring the things you always wear. For me, that means familiar denim, a white tee or henley, a favorite sweater, and good boots; pieces that make it easy to get dressed in combinations that already feel like me.

#6951. The 63 Best New Perfumes and Fragrances of 2026

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

We sniffed, we spritzed, we sprayed. Only the perfumes below left a lingering impression, earning them a spot on our annual best-of list.

Everything smells like vanilla. At least, that’s what perfume naysayers will tell you. And it’s somewhat true: If you’re judging by what’s popular, ubiquitous, or cool with the youth, then sure—vanilla scents and sweet gourmands are still trending.

But hundreds of other fragrance launches in 2026 deserve a spot on your vanity and a spritz on your skin, and many are far less sweet. Gourmands took a turn for the weird and fruity this year, with notes like lychee and rice milk popping up in otherwise innocuous formulas. Woods and ambers continued to reign, as did spice-forward and citrus scents with unexpected depth. Perfumers are meeting our demand for newness in increasingly interesting, artistic ways, and we smelled hundreds of their creations to choose the 63 winners of our annual fragrance awards.

See all the winners of the 2026 Harper’s Bazaar Fragrance Awards, plus quotes from our beauty editors on what makes these formulas worthy of your next blind buy.

“Perfumer Olivier Polge reinvented the beloved “Coco Mad” scent as Crush Absolu, a new juice centered around patchouli, amber, rose, and a lychee-grapefruit accord. ‘There’s something voluptuous, something very prideful, something very addictive,’ Polge says about the new scent.”

“The new iteration of J’adore was inspired by Rihanna herself. As such, it’s a sweeter, bolder take, with notes of honeyed jasmine, rose, ylang-ylang, and vanilla.” —JR

"Bazaar’s Beauty Commerce Editor, Tiffany Dodson Davis, describes Guerlain’s L’Heure Bleue Eau de Parfum as the “scent of sunset,” thanks to a blue-sky accord that evokes the moment the sun goes down and the air turns crisp, clean, and fresh." —KI

Burberry Goddess Amber Vanilla Eau de Parfum Intense

“Lavender, maple syrup, four vanilla notes—I feel like a dog hearing all my favorite words. The result is a warm, ambery gourmand perfume that’s perfect for the cooler months.” —JR

Armani Beauty Emporio Armani Power of You Eau de Parfum

"Passionfruit adds an unexpected zing to the vanilla base here, making Power of You one of our favorite tropical-leaning gourmands of the year. It's pure summertime sex appeal." —JR

“Henry Rose Ripe captures fruits—from lychee to watermelon—at their juiciest, ripest point, like a delectable first bite, before packing a punch with black pepper, cardamom, and powdery florals.” —Katie Intner, beauty market and commerce editor

“One of the most unexpected fruity florals on the list, Influxious Magic Hour melds watermelon and dragon fruit with spicy chili pepper for a burst of juicy energy.” —KI

Balmain Paris Destin De Balmain Eau de Parfum

"The opening note of Destin is strawberry, a modern and sweet fruity note that quickly dries down to a patchouli-forward, gorgeous floral musk. It will make you smell expensive." —JR

"Neroli, bergamot, and yuzu combine with notes of orange blossom, jasmine, and tonka bean for a well-rounded fragrance that stands out from the pack. Parfum de Marly's Athénaïs eau de parfum has a zesty composition upon first spritz before drying down to a powdery finish." —Tiffany Dodson Davis, beauty commerce editor

“Gap recently brought back its iconic fragrance line from the ’90s, including the nostalgic Grass Eau de Parfum. It now features matcha, vetiver, green mandarin, and cedar for a fresh take on its outdoorsy, earthy scent.” —KI

Maison Francis Kurkdjian Oud Velvet Mood Extrait de Parfum

#6949. A New Book Explores the Lessons We Can All Learn From Indigenous Wellness Traditions

The global wellness industry has reached a fever pitch—and shows no signs of slowing down. Overstimulated, under-rested, and on the brink of burnout, we’re desperately seeking antidotes to the ills of the digital era. But what if the answers to our modern-day woes lie in ancient Native American wisdom, not in a supposed cure-all gadget, supplement, or fitness routine?

For millennia, Indigenous communities have prioritized connection to nature, practiced reciprocity with community, and upheld sacred cultural traditions. We can all embrace these holistic principles in our daily lives, says Lakota/Anishinaabe wellness advocate Chelsey Luger. That’s the premise behind her new book, Native Home: The Ancestral Art of Creating Healing Spaces. In it, she offers tangible ways to incorporate these concepts into our living spaces to promote mental, physical, spiritual, and emotional balance.

“Everybody in North America should understand Native American home teachings because we’re all living on this land,” says Luger, who is an enrolled member of the Turtle Mountain Band of Chippewa and a descendant of the Standing Rock Sioux Tribe. “There’s a movement in the home industry right now centered around ensuring our surroundings support our well-being. This might be an emerging conversation in the wellness space, but Indigenous communities have been living this way for centuries.”

Luger and her husband, Onk Akimel O’odham fitness educator Thosh Collins, co-founded the grassroots initiative Well For Culture back in 2013, with the aim of revitalizing Indigenous wellness practices. Since then, they’ve been traveling to tribal nations across the country to both teach and learn from these communities. Native Home is the culmination of those lessons.

She underscores that these principles are applicable to everyone, no matter their heritage, with this caveat: The difference between appreciation and appropriation is all in the approach. “I’m not encouraging you to do exactly what Indigenous communities do,” she says. “I’m encouraging you to apply these teachings to your own home in a way that reflects your own culture.”

For example, in a Native worldview, every home should tell a story through its design and decor, Luger explains. Her ancestors painted their tepees with images representing their familial experiences. Similarly, the artwork adorning our walls should feel curated and specific to our identity.

With this in mind, Luger encourages people to stay intimately involved in their home’s design and decorating process, as she’s doing with her family’s in-the-works house on Arizona’s Salt River Reservation (where Collins is from). But you needn’t be building a new abode to reap the benefits of Indigenous wellness. There are easy, everyday ways to embrace these principles—and they don’t cost a thing.

Starting small is as simple as decluttering one room to reduce sensory overload, letting in more natural sunlight to balance circadian rhythms, opening up your windows for cross-drafting airflow, or carving out an area for movement—even if it’s only a designated stretching space. Next-level applications encompass philosophies like Indigenous minimalism and Native geomancy (auspicious arrangement, akin to feng shui), which are all explained in the book.

Like most Indigenous wisdom, Luger’s advice is pretty intuitive—and yet, it isn’t how we lead our lives today. “We might say that we don’t want to overconsume, but Americans tend to think the bigger the house, the better,” she says. “Our generation is facing a housing crisis, and we should all embrace smaller living. Not only is it easier to maintain, but it keeps us better connected. That’s what this is ultimately about: interconnection.”

Here, Luger offers five tips for incorporating Indigenous wellness in your home.

“We’ve always had a respect for the elements—air, fire, water, and earth. Air in particular is considered sacred by most Indigenous communities. Here in the United States, we use air conditioning more than most other countries in the world. It’s a wonderful luxury, but our indoor environments should better reflect the outdoors. Try keeping your windows open for part of the day, no matter the season.”

“Doorways are considered portals, which are places of high energy. Growing up, my dad would hang a hand drum or a medicine wheel above our door. It’s a way to bring good energy both for those arriving and for those leaving. Honor the doorway by hanging something that represents the presence of your ancestors and offers both protection and peace.”

“It’s a very European idea to have a separate room for everything. In contrast, Indigenous homes historically tended to be more fluid, with communal rooms that foster togetherness. That interconnection is part of our values as Indigenous people. Let at least one space in your home feel more multi-use, where family members can share space.”

“Many traditional Native structures are round, which represents women, Mother Earth, and our egalitarian values. Indigenous architect Douglas Cardinal (Métis/Blackfoot), who designed the National Museum of the American Indian in Washington, D.C., often expresses this with curvilinear forms. But you don’t have to build a round house or room. Choose a circular dining table, rugs, and pillows rather than square or rectangular ones.”

“As Native people, we’ve never lost that closeness to our ancestors, even through colonization and the boarding school era. That spiritual and practical connection to who we are as people is just part of our culture. Whoever you are, wherever you come from—everybody has ancestors they can and should feel connected to. Take time to learn about your culture, and then make your home a reflection of that.”

#6943. The 10 Best Lessons I Learned From My First Year Living Alone

The other night, while I was two glasses deep into a bottle of natural wine, sitting on my couch in my Hollywood studio in front of The Simpsons, when I wondered: had either of my grandmothers ever had the opportunity to live like this? Alone but (mostly) not lonely, in a space completely their own, with nobody vying for their labor or attention or filling the silence? I moved into my own place just over a year ago after a breakup with a live-in partner, and while I hesitate to go full loneliness influencer, I’m grateful for the series of privileges and twists of fate that have brought me to the highs, lows and everyday mediums of solo living.

It turns out Virginia Woolf wasn’t lying when she wrote about the importance of a woman having a room of her own, or, as Destiny’s Child so memorably put it: “The watch I'm wearing (I bought it), the house I live in (I bought it), the car I'm driving (I bought it), I depend on me (I depend on me).” To be fair, I rent my studio, I don’t wear a watch, and my 2013 Hyundai Sonata is covered in dents, but the motto still applies. Below are ten lessons I learned from my first year living alone in Los Angeles.

I’ve garnered a not-unearned reputation among my friends for bailing a little more frequently than I should, and while that’s a bad habit no matter who you cohabitate with, living alone has taught me that I need to treat my social engagements—whether they’re set-in-stone events or casual movie dates—with the same commitment I bring to work obligations. My friends deserve it, and just as importantly, I deserve to see and enjoy myself amongst actual human beings every day, especially now that I can’t count on automatic social interaction at home.

Do you own a drill? Me neither. When I moved into my studio last fall, I told myself I would become the kind of person who initiated her own D.I.Y. projects and fixed things when they broke without a) crying or b) live-documenting it on Instagram—but as it turns out, I’m still me, and even the newfound independence of solo living hasn’t made me handier. I’d like to build my skills in this arena, but until I do, I've realized that it’s a lot easier and more joy-inducing to pay a helpful friend I refer to as my “handybutch” to hang my art, install my Tulip shades and turn on my pilot light than it is to farm out the jobs to a random TaskRabbit…or sit around waiting for myself to suddenly become amazing at using a level. Sure, money’s tighter now that I don’t split bills with a roommate, but investing in own comfort in my living space feels worth it.

I’ve always known this to be true when it comes to fashion (don’t talk to me unless I’ve got a proper ‘fit on…literally, please do not), but when I lived with roommates or my ex, it was easier for me to surrender control of my living environment—a.k.a. be a slob—and only complete the amount of chores that resulted in nobody passive-aggressively texting me to please clean the bathroom. Now that I live alone, I’m the only one affected by my messiness, and as it turns out, I value myself enough to try to combat it where possible, whether that means making my bed every day for the first time in my life or doing my dishes immediately so that I don’t attract bugs.

On that bug note…yes, I’m more of a proponent of the ‘pay someone to do the hard things’ theory of solo living, but there are some hard things you can’t and arguably shouldn’t outsource, and for me, Hard Thing #1 is definitely dealing with insects. I’ve been lucky enough to only ever spot two water bugs in my apartment over the last year (which a very patient exterminator told me did not “constitute an infestation”), but both times, I wept and dry-heaved and instinctually went through my mental Rolodex of who I could call to kill them before…growing the hell up and killing them myself. Do I particularly want to put this new life skill into play a third time? No, but at least I know I can if I have to.

Okay, I stole this quote from Joan Didion, but when you live alone, the days and nights can collapse into themselves without a real sense of occasion…unless you proactively create said occasion, something I try to do every time I light an expensive candle or stick of incense instead of “saving it for later” or start chopping onions for that complex Bolognese recipe I’ve been putting off trying because it seems like too much work to feed just one person. Invest in some good Tupperware for leftovers, light the candle, live your damn life!

Who knew a game-show lifeline could also function as a mental-health life preserver? When I first moved into my place alone, I worried that my lifelong tendency to self-isolate when things are hard would flare up without someone to physically share space with, but I’ve actually gotten better about reaching out when I need support, maybe because it’s just me and my dog (on the nights he’s not spending with his dad, that is). I still have some avoidant tendencies, but calling my besties to vent when solo living and my currently single status make me feel lonely has helped me realize that real emotional connection isn’t just about someone being there: It’s about letting someone be there for you, and doing your best to be there for them in return.

As it turns out, I’m kind of guarded about bringing dates over to my place; partly because a reactive, bark-y Maltipoo who hates strangers lives there half the time, but also because I’ve become unexpectedly protective of my domain. When I lived with a rotating cast of roommates in my twenties, it felt like no big deal to entertain a near-stranger in a bedroom that invariably consisted of a bare mattress, a stack of books and an empty iced-latte cup, but now that I’ve put time and energy into creating a home I love living alone, I have to really want someone to visit if I invite them over. (Don’t worry, I’m still making regrettable romantic choices every so often, I just go to their places instead of mine.)

I always assumed I’d get bored living alone without other people’s gossip and drama to entertain me. Sometimes I do, and you know what? It’s kind of great. Quitting weed has taught me that I don’t need to fill every dull moment with stimulation or numb out to avoid hard feelings, and living alone while weed-abstinent is a consistent exercise in filling time (although less of one than I feared it would be, given the whole “no canceling social plans” thing). Boredom has led me to start drawing again for the first time since I was a kid, perfect my French onion soup recipe, and even start doing stand-up at mercifully low-attendance open mics, so where else will it lead me, I wonder?

I am a devoted estate-sale and flea-market freak who’s lowkey addicted to trinkets, and while I’m trying to curb my spending (I need to pay my handybutch, after all), I’ve noticed that the cheapest items I own—from a vintage copy of Mary Ellman’s Thinking About Women anthology that a kind bookseller at Womb House Books gifted me to a $5 milk frother I scored during a MoMA Design Store sale to a $10 sculptural yellow dish I scored at the Long Beach Flea Market—are the ones that I tend to look at and sigh in contentment, thrilled to live in my very own space without having to accommodate anyone else’s items or design ideas. Minimalism is overrated!

I’ve always loved Ruth Reichl’s description of making herself a bare-bones omelet in a borrowed apartment in Paris and “thinking that I was being a guest to myself” in her 2001 memoir Comfort Me with Apples, and I try to embody that spirit every time I run a bath or choose a movie or make a meal in my studio, whether it’s a multi-step chili or a simple bowl of yogurt with fruit and Tamar Adler’s granola. I may not be able to host a crowd in my studio the way I could in previous living spaces, but that’s okay; as it turns out, I’m enough.

#6941. ‘Off Campus’ Season 2 Might Make a Major Departure From the Books, Thanks to This Casting Shakeup

The absence could change a key storyline from the books in the Prime Video series.

Fans are already counting down the days until we see Allie and Dean’s love story pan out on Off Campus season 2, and I don’t blame them one bit. It’ll be a minute until we reunite with the Briar U gang, but fans are keeping up with every update on the TV adaptation of Elle Kennedy’s book series in the meantime. And the latest news on season 2 is that a certain cast member won’t be returning.

“I’m not gonna be in the next season,” Riley Davis, who plays Allie’s ex-boyfriend Sean on the Prime Video series, told People at the New York City premiere of his movie November 1963 on November 18.

As for the reason behind his exit? Riley says there were some scheduling conflicts—which checks out, since he has a role in another YA series, Netflix’s Crew Girl. It’s unclear whether that particular project played a part in his departure from Off Campus.

“There was a plan for some Sean in season 2, and we just had a bit of a scheduling thing on my side and a bit on their side,” he explained. “So we’ll see what happens down the line.”

What’s more: Riley’s exit could potentially signal that the second season of Off Campus might go a different direction from the book series it’s based on.

As viewers saw in the show’s first season, Riley’s character, Sean, plays a pivotal role in Allie and Dean’s romance. In the books, he and Allie have an on-again, off-again relationship—but we might not see that pan out now that Riley is out.

This isn’t the first time the TV adaptation shook things up with some changes from the source material. In season 1, the show’s writers introduced John Logan’s nonbinary sibling Jules Logan, who noticeably wasn’t in the OG book series.

We’ll have to see how Riley/Sean’s absence is really handled when Off Campus season 2 hits Prime Video on April 28, 2027!

#6939. Supersized Fashion: The Rise of Giant Bags, Jewelry, and Accessories

Rosalía was recently spotted walking through the New York City streets with a Dana Toder bag over three feet long. Paired with her vintage pink satin ballet platform heels from Junko Shimada, the massive, hulking bag from the emerging designer was the kind of shocking jolt of life and newness that typical fashion paparazzi shots lack.

In case there was any doubt, the point of view was the bag. Supersized bags are ubiquitous right now: Chanel, Acne Studios, and Bottega Veneta all showcased their own versions for fall/winter 2026, but Toder’s took the idea to a new level with its extreme size and concept. Consider the bags just one of the many examples of supersizing the everyday. Chandelier earrings and oversize necklaces have never been so big at Saint Laurent and Valentino. Elsewhere, labels like Comme des Garçons, Vaquera, and Givenchy amped up their usual voluminous silhouettes even more for spring/summer 2027. Rising designer Beata Rydbacken is also making the biggest hair clips you’ve ever seen in your entire life.

There’s something undoubtedly beautiful about taking an everyday object and making it obscenely huge. It puts a focus on what some may consider the everyday or mundane and makes it sort of beautifully ludicrous. Dana Toder originally designed her “Baggage Bag” for Krähe, a fashion film by Lili Conzen, shot on 16mm, which will be released in spring 2027. Accompanied by exhibitions in London, Paris, Berlin, and Vienna, which will also showcase the bag, the film questions who gets to be seen in fashion and gives older women the leading roles. “When Lili shared the film’s concept with me, it connected really well with how I see clothes and accessories: as things that go through life with us and hold memories and emotions,” Toder tells ELLE. “I see a woman’s relationship with her bag or her garments as something very emotional and complex. The focus on older women felt especially meaningful when thinking about how those relationships develop over a lifetime.”

For Toder, it’s also a representation of metaphorical and physical baggage. The print featured on the big bag combines analog photographs from the ’80s to today, taken by the designer, director, and actresses in the film, and plays a role in showcasing the shared experience across different women, ages, and times. The bag itself was also made with the help of her 80-year-old leatherworking mentor, Shmulik.

“I’m interested in everything that becomes emotionally attached to a bag: the things it holds, the places it has been with us, and how the material changes over time. It might hold an object that reminds you of someone, or the bag itself might remind you of a certain period in your life,” she says.

“We carry all of that around too. I’m always carrying so much stuff, and I love the sight of a woman digging through her bag, with things falling out. I honestly think it’s one of the most beautiful sights. You’re looking for your keys and suddenly going through things that hold so many memories. Some might have been there for two years, or tucked away in your wallet for what feels like forever. I find something very emotional in that everyday moment, coming across parts of your life that you carry without really thinking about them.”

Seeing the familiar through a campy, oversize lens gives one both feelings of nostalgia and whimsy, and that’s perhaps why we’re experiencing so many humungous accessories lately. In the case of Rydbacken, in addition to her giant hair clips, she’s also made supersized wired earbuds, the biggest studded belt to ever exist, and jumbo pins, with many going viral. In fashion’s current game of scale, the bigger the accessory, the more room it leaves for memory, meaning, and a little bit of spectacle.

#6935. Who Is Daddy Franklin From ‘The Body’? For Starters, Nnamdi Asomugha Is Kerry Washington’s Husband

Daddy Franklin is married to Olivia Pope?

If you watched The Body in one sitting and are now fully obsessed with it, you’re not alone!! The horrifying coming-of-age series is scary good, and while you may not be familiar with the actor who plays Father Samuel Franklin (aka Daddy Franklin), you definitely know his very famous wife. As it turns out, Nnamdi Asomugha has been married to Kerry Washington for over 13 years now, having fallen in love after watching Kerry perform in Race on Broadway in 2010. Kerry later confirmed the story during a 2018 interview with Marie Claire, saying, “The last time I did theater, it completely transformed my life. That’s where I met my husband.” Cute!!!

Though things got off to a rocky start (Kerry apparently told Nnamdi she didn’t date athletes, and Nnamdi told Kerry he didn’t date actors), the two got together anyway, and just about three years later in June 2013, they tied the knot in a super low-key ceremony. According to their marriage license, Kerry and Nnamdi said “I do” in Hailey, Idaho, where Nnamdi’s sister officiated and Kerry’s parents served as witnesses. Since then, the couple has welcomed two kids together—Isabelle Amarachi, born in 2014, and Caleb Kelechi, born in 2016—and are seemingly thriving outside of the spotlight. But we think it’s time to learn a lil bit more about Nnamdi, don’t you?

Here’s everything you should know about the man behind Daddy Franklin, including how he rose to fame as a former NFL cornerback and what he’s up to next.

After growing up in Louisiana in a Nigerian Igbo household, Nnamdi attended the University of California, Berkeley, where he played for the California Golden Bears football team. Soon after graduating, Nnamdi was drafted in the first round of the 2003 NFL Draft by the Oakland Raiders, which is very impressive.

Over his 11-year professional football career, Nnamdi also played for the Philadelphia Eagles and the San Francisco 49ers, and in December 2013, the cornerback signed a one-day contract with Oakland so he could retire as a Raider. I love to see a loyal man!

According to Nnamdi, the decision to retire at 32 was difficult, but it still felt like the right time for him. “Yeah, I felt that it was a good time, but it didn’t make it any easier,” he told UPROXX. “It’s a game that 'til this day I miss, and I miss playing. It’s not that I want to go play again, but I miss so many aspects of it; being around the team, being around the coaching, that calm before the storm.”

Nnamdi first caught the acting bug all the way back in 2008, when he landed the role of “Party Guest with Wife” on the CW sitcom The Game. (Remember: There are no small roles, only small actors!!) He then caught the eye of director Peter Berg on the set of a Nike commercial shoot in 2009, who was impressed by the athlete’s ease in front of the camera. From there, Nnamdi went on to play small roles in Friday Night Lights, Leverage, and Cubed before making his on-screen feature film debut in 2012’s Fire with Fire.

Since retiring from the NFL, Nnamdi has fully committed himself to his acting career with starring roles in the films Crown Heights, Sylvie’s Love, The Good Nurse, The Knife, and—most recently—The Body. He’s also served as a producer on several of those projects as well.

Despite all his success, Nnamdi still remains as modest as ever, avoiding social media and press to instead focus on his craft. “I’m really bad at self-promotion. I know that about myself,” he previously explained to Bleacher Report back in 2017. “People told me all of the time, ‘You could be such a big star if you just talked about yourself more,’ but I’m not good at that. It’s always been about team.”

During a 2018 interview with Today, Kerry corrected a host when they alluded to her two children, saying, “I’m a mother of three." The reason: Nnamdi has a now-teenage daughter from another relationship, who Kerry has helped raise alongside her two biological children. And just like with Isabelle and Caleb, the couple keeps their eldest far from the paparazzi, so very little is known about her.

As Kerry joked to People, “I have moments where I take pictures or videos of one of my three amazing kids and I wanna post it online, and I tend to just send it to my parents or my shrink instead.”

Philanthropy is important to Nnamdi, which is why he created the Asomugha Foundation in 2005 at the very beginning of his career.

The foundation has two main programs: Orphans and Widows in Need (which provides food, shelter, medicine, vocational training, literacy efforts, and scholarships to widows and orphans in Nigeria) and Asomugha College Tour for Scholars (which takes selected students from Bay Area and Los Angeles area high schools on college tours across the country).

Though Kerry and Nnamdi are both actors and producers, it isn’t often that they work together on creative projects. Back in 2017, Nnamdi told Vanity Fair that it was possible he and his wife would co-produce a project at some point.

“We would produce,” he said. “We’d probably produce before acting, I would think.” The following year, his production company, iAm21 Entertainment, helped produce American Son, a Broadway play starring Kerry, and hopefully, that won’t be the last time the two are involved in the same project.

As far as sharing the screen goes, however, Nnamdi doesn’t think that will ever happen. As he told The New York Times, “I produced American Son, but as actors, there’s no plan as of now for that collaboration. We’re very supportive of each other’s journeys, but we’ve always been that way. We always want the best for each other in whatever we’re doing.”

Case in point? The couple’s *chef’s kiss* appearance at the 2025 Met Gala. Ahem:

Oh, and the duo’s 2025 Golden Globes looks:

And ofc, he was right by Kerry's side during her December 2024 Hollywood Walk of Fame ceremony, where she finally received her well-deserved star. How cute do they look with Kerry's parents??

TL;DR: Kerry and Nnamdi truly couldn’t be any cuter. Keep on being adorably low-key, you two.
