#6932. The Scoop with Moynat’s Aydha Mehnaz: On Launching Her New Consultancy Venture Avari

Welcome to the Scoop: a weekly email series in which I quiz fashion insiders on the stories of the week. This will be a way for the Vogue Business community to synthesize and reflect on the latest headlines and get a little inside scoop every Friday.

This week’s guest is Aydha Mehnaz, whose day job is head of global brand relations at Moynat, purveyor of trunks and the monogrammed totes your fashionable colleague started carrying around in the last couple of years. I met Aydha when she worked in the PR department at Mugler, alongside Bertrand Le Gall (now the image director at Moynat), while I was at Selfridges. I remember it was the 30th anniversary of the brand’s Angel perfume that we were working on.

Aydha with Paris Hilton at Mugler SS24 where Paris made her Paris runway debut.

We hit it off because Aydha is cool and ambitious in all the right ways. She grew up in Bangladesh and studied biochemistry before leaving it all behind about 10 years ago for a Masters at ESMOD in Paris. From there, she landed at Mugler but, in between all that, she also started one of the first and best Tumblrs on modest fashion, called Minazification. The blog doesn’t work anymore, but Aydha very much does. She’s recently started a new venture, which is what we are talking about today.

I recently launched Avari, my communications consultancy for brands and talent who want to reach the Southeastern consumer or Southeastern brands who want to do the opposite. I built it with my brother, who is based in Dhaka, because it was important for us to operate in four different time zones.

I come from Bangladesh but moved to Paris about 10 years ago, and now I am in New York. Throughout my career, while moving through those luxury spaces, I’ve been very conscious of how those places shape me, and Avari has the same geographical footprint as I do. The word in Sanskrit speaks to something that’s obscure, almost like a shapeshifter. Luxury no longer holds the same values it once did because the consumer has changed and has a more multicultural, constantly changing attitude.

Aydha with Jordan Clarkson of the NY Knicks during the Moynat Kasing Lung shoot.

We want to be a partner for brands and talents, making sure you can become whoever you want. For me, the main objective is to nurture talent from South Asia. There isn’t enough representation in Western media, and for those who are there, there isn’t enough guidance on how to package themselves to enter the Western market. There is so much talent coming from that part of the world, and particularly from Bangladesh since the July Uprising of 2024, but few know where to look or how. I do.

Talk to me about the difference between the Bangladesh you left as a student and the Bangladesh you are building a business for 10 years later.

When I left, the creative pool was quite small. There weren’t many people doing new things; it was more iterations of what you would see from India or from Pakistan. Now there are so many designers, chefs, photographers, and all sorts of other artists who see themselves as global talents rather than being limited by their surroundings. This is why, for Avari, it was so important to set up a hub in Dhaka.

Aydha with guests attending at Moynat’s Travelling Popup in Chicago.

You are the first Bangladeshi on the Scoop, so I have to ask you about the factory protests. What can you tell us about what is really happening on the ground?

This is my very own observation, and I am not speaking for anyone, but protests are not new in Bangladesh or anywhere. Protests happen in Paris too, right? And the truth is always somewhere in the middle. In Bangladesh, you have a set of factories that are not regulated. They probably treat their workers very poorly and keep costs low by avoiding taking safety measures. But then you also have factories that are actually charging you a premium price, giving you the polo or the Carhartt sweatshirt you wear every day. This sweater I am wearing is actually made in Bangladesh.

So those factories are providing healthcare and safe facilities for their employees. The people who don’t get the right treatment are obviously going to protest. But the thing is that whenever these conversations take place in Western media, only one side, the bad side of Bangladesh, is shown. And there are also local people who profit from exacerbating that picture.

Of course, the memes are crazy. I like the one about Robert Pattinson getting all the jobs.

It’s interesting how much things have changed in 10-20 years. When I was growing up in Greece, global news didn’t have the same effect, whereas now we all see the same memes at the same time, everywhere. But we are all still culturally different.

Right, and that has affected how global consumers behave. As an example, Bangkok for the Bangladeshi is considered a second Dhaka — you literally walk on the street and run into people you know. Because the high earners from South Asia go to shop in Thailand, but also in India, the UAE, America, and Europe. South Asians have so many cultural similarities, and we often speak each other’s language too.

My introduction to luxury wasn’t the Western brands but my mum’s heirloom jewelry that she is waiting for me to inherit. We are talking about 24-karat pieces that have been passed down from generation to generation. You can see how a brand like Sabyasachi brings that into the modern world, and there are so many other local creatives with a global view. Locals are finally investing in South Asian culture and South Asian art. With Avari, the idea is: how do we fit into that?

Applications for the 2027 LVMH prize are now open. Who would you like to see apply?

Khanums, a British label with Bangladeshi roots, and Menka from Bangladesh, who make these handwoven sarees with heirloom weavers. Also, Kartik Research from India is fab, but he’s already taken part.

#6931. 7 Holiday Decor Trends Designers Say We're About to See Everywhere

The pros are leaning into handmade holiday style.

Pictured above: A holiday-ready space by designer Marie Flanigan.

Christmas is just around the corner, and as the holiday spirit makes its way into our homes, it’s a good time to give your seasonal decor a refresh. This year, on-trend doesn’t have to mean brand-new—designers love decorating with heirloom pieces collected over time.

However, if you’re in the mood to try something a little different, here are seven trends on our radar for a chic Christmas.

This winter, our decor is taking a note from European culture—again. Swedish folk art is emerging as a hot winter trend in both holiday and home decor. Defined by bold red hues, tulip motifs, and S-scrolls, the style blends cozy-core with the heritage style craze.

“European folk influences are so big right now,” says designer Johanna Lyle of Jo Lyle & Co. “Think Swedish Dala horses, quilt motifs, and decorative stitching. I think we will see those continue into holiday decor.”

When it comes to her tree decor, interior designer Francesca Grace waves a hand at the carefully contrived, and she’s not alone. “My ornaments are collected from my travels, so the tree is really a collection of memories. I love unpacking them and remembering where each one came from. They don’t all match, and I wouldn’t want them to,” says Grace. “I let the ornaments lead. That mix feels much more personal to me, and it gives the tree something new to say as the collection grows.” This year’s trends hinge on antiqued, inherited style, with one-off pieces that channel bold character and hardly ever come with a matching set.

Interior designer Marie Flanigan likes to add a fresh twist to her seasonal decor with dried oranges and pops of cranberry red.

“We've seen such a beautiful resurgence of dried citrus in holiday decorating over the past few years,” Flanigan explains. “And I think cranberries are ready for their moment to shine. Their rich red hue feels especially festive, and I love the idea of incorporating them into fresh greenery, draping them across a mantel, or pairing them with dried oranges for a more layered look.”

If there’s one classic Christmas element Grace hopes makes a comeback, it's multicolored lights. Inherently nostalgic, these bold-hued bulbs were a ’90s staple.

“I’m ready for a little less restraint on the tree. Those reds, blues, greens, and yellows immediately bring back the feeling of Christmas as a child. They also pull together a collection of ornaments that might otherwise seem completely unrelated. White lights can be beautiful, but I want my Christmas tree to have some mischief,” says Grace.

The classic red-and-green combination may take the title for the most iconic holiday palette, but this year, color trends are going complementary. While base colors stay warm and slightly rusted, patterned pieces are taking on cooler tones. This year, we’re also trading traditional plaid for Scottish tartan.

“Warm crimsons, rich browns, tactile fabrics, and blue-green plaids make a beautiful holiday palette,” says Elizabeth Bennett, cofounder of the interior design firm Kobel + Co.

In a total rejection of mass-manufactured everything, handmade felt pieces are taking holiday homes by storm. The style has even coined a new holiday aesthetic: #Kitschmas, and, according to Lyle, it’s here to stay. “As AI continues to homogenize design, things made by humans will feel more special and important. Handmade and crafted items create stories around decor that feel meaningful and can be treasured year after year,” she says.

Moody browns are steadily emerging as the new neutral as we inch closer to the new year, and lucky for our winter design schemes, the hue really is as versatile as it seems. Paired with powdery blues, bold crimsons, and even minty greens, chocolate brown grounds nearly any palette and matches every metallic hue.

“I’m particularly drawn to chocolate brown as an unexpected foundation for holiday color palettes. It's incredibly rich and versatile. I love the idea of moving beyond traditional red and green and allowing the existing colors of a room to inform the holiday decor. You can create something incredibly festive through tonal layering, subtle metallic accents, and variations in material,” Flanigan explains.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6927. Wig Business Is Big Business

The hair category is currently a hotbed for growth. Alongside much innovation in existing products, the past decade has seen hair transplants, scalp serums, and novel treatment concepts like head spas surge in popularity. And the category holds appeal across many demographics, from the more traditional mid-life market to burgeoning legions of ‘looksmaxxers’ and ever younger consumers seeking interventions for early-stage thinning, pattern baldness and GLP1-induced hair loss. While many consumers are investing in supplements, serums or aesthetic treatments to address the issue, another cohort is spending big on extensions and wigs.

After 40 years in hairdressing, hairstylist and salon owner Charlotte Mensah has seen trends come and go, but notes that extensions have had enduring appeal. “Even with my clients that embrace their natural look, many are seeing value in adding volume/length and will request a weft that matches their hair texture to achieve this,” she explains. What’s more, extensions and wigs have become less of a secret, considered desirable across race, gender and other consumer groups. “The appearance of full, healthy hair is always fashionable. These days, we have new methods like tapes and synthetic fibers that can simulate human hair, making hair extensions more accessible to everyone,” she adds.

“Full hair has always been in style,” says Charlotte Mensah. “I’ve noticed clients are increasingly interested in extensions that mirror their hair’s natural texture.”

As revenues go, the wig business is a lucrative business. The SKUs in this sector — wigs, wefts and other hair extension systems, such as lace toupees and semi-permanent, bonded K-tips — commonly necessitate multibuy purchases to achieve desired hairstyles and individually command price tags upwards of £250.

At the top end of the market, a single wig or set of extensions can sell for tens of thousands, with long extension lengths, made-to-measure customizations and laborious finishes justifying costs. Value perception is high, thanks to human hair’s product lifespan, reusability and styling versatility. Together with growing hair loss concerns, these factors encourage consumers to adopt an investment-purchase mindset that makes for higher average order values in the extensions space.

Having earned £20,000 through 30 orders in just 10 minutes on launch day, CEO Denise Nwogbu has honed a strategy for success here. Her wig brand Go Glueless positions its product offer as a “hair appointment in a box”, eliminating the friction associated with installing specialist extensions. Customers are drawn to the convenience and seamless nature of the wigs, which do not require adhesives to style — instead, they can be secured through elastic headbands on the wig’s interior that keep it fitted in place on the user’s scalp. She says this ease of installation has driven high waitlist demand and often results in sellouts across the range on drop days.

Denise Nwogbu, CEO and founder of Go Glueless, has found success positioning her product as a “salon appointment in a box.”

“We limit releases to four times a year and use a pre-order model. This allows time to carefully source single-donor, virgin hair, then handcraft and customize each bespoke unit,” Nwogbu shares. “Provenance is everything — I chose my supplier in Vietnam because she has a real relationship with each woman who grows and sells their hair for the production of the extensions. She does not delegate to hair collectors, she goes personally to the villages to find willing volunteers that have not been coerced into cutting their hair. She can tell you their exact age, name and lifestyle, and pays them fairly with an understanding of virgin hair value in the West.”

By the time the hair reaches Go Glueless headquarters in Birmingham, it has been cuticle-tested to ensure quality and longevity. Then, a team of wig specialists construct, color and cut units to exact customer measurements. Attracting an affluent, often time-poor clientele of professional women and public figures, Go Glueless’s USP lies in doing the labor so the end consumer has it completely removed.

On launch day, Go Glueless made £20,000 in online preorders. Now, the brand has opened its first IRL space —a suite where customers can browse and get bespoke fittings for their wigs.

“It’s a salon experience with no skills needed, but education is also important: every customer gets access to exclusive tutorials through GG Academy, our online portal,” Denise says. This in itself is a clever strategy, attracting large audiences already searching for terms like “wig install tutorial”, which has amassed 478,000 impressions on TikTok. “And we educate our clients about help available to them if they are buying wigs to manage medical hair loss — many with conditions like alopecia or cancer aren’t aware of their entitlement to VAT relief.”

In the wig business, hair loss sufferers are likely to be engaged, repeat customers, presenting major sales opportunities. But profits aren’t the only piece of the puzzle — the most impactful brands focus on corporate social responsibility, too.

Market leader Beauty Works, whose assortment includes both professional-grade extensions and DIY clip-ins, is a case in point. Its relationship with the charity Little Lady Locks, which provides free medical wigs and hair services to minors with medical hair loss, is symbiotic: the charity benefits from Beauty Works’s financial investment, resources and expert volunteers, while Beauty Works can exhibit purpose-driven values, benefiting from enhanced brand image and positive engagement within aligned communities.

Beauty Works donates hair extensions to Little Lady Locks, helping the charity provide free wigs to boost the confidence of hair loss sufferers under 18.

Elsewhere, the brand is big on avoiding ethical quandaries common to the hair trade by enforcing strict compliance throughout its supply chain. “Our human hair is voluntarily sold by individuals who are fairly compensated,” says Randilyn Dela Cruz, product architect and vice president of development at Beauty Works’ parent company BIG. “Our sourcing standards require a respectful process and prohibit child labor, forced labor and sourcing from regions where there are known human-rights violations,” she details.

Sourcing is an important aspect for any wig business to get right, especially given a black market of sorts exists. A far cry from the luxury products they’re promoted as in the West, some vendors acquire hair of dubious origin, obscure its imperfections and sell to unwitting customers overseas at a high markup. An ITV News exposé found this can include waste hair pulled from sewer drains in India, as well as “floor hair” collected from the ground of salons by cheap laborers desperate to cash in on the lucrative trade.

“It’s ‘the fast fashion’ side of wigs,” Denise stresses, going on to explain that these vendors chemically relax the waste balls of hair — which are made up of several cut hairs with different properties because they are from multiple donors’ heads — to the point where it appears flawless. “They often then market it deceptively under names like ‘bone straight’. “You couldn’t tell with the naked eye, but when you use that kind of hair to produce hair extensions, time always tells. It frizzes up, tangles easily and is unpredictable after the chemical coatings wash off, and business owners risk having that happen in the hands of your consumer if you don’t do your due diligence at the point of sourcing,” she warns.

Hence, Denise insists on flying to Vietnam personally to inspect and test the quality of any hair used in the making of Go Glueless units. It’s expensive work, but something she recommends as worthwhile for new businesses hoping to break into the luxury wig space.

Another common source of hair that can pose ethical dilemmas is Indian temple hair. This hair comes from Hindu tonsuring ceremonies, in which some donors shave their heads under the religious belief that it releases past spiritual baggage. Others agree to shed their hair as a sacred sacrifice to be devoted to specific deities. These individuals are often left in the dark about the final destination of their hair and its huge monetary value if it is later sold on to wig companies at auction, and they often receive no part of the profits, according to a recent report from the BBC. Questions around exploitation in these cases can influence perception among discerning end consumers, and are worth investigating to reduce brand risk.

Model wears Synths 2: an extension that uses collagen-based fibers to add length and volume without the need for human hair or more toxic synthetic alternatives.

To sidestep sourcing issues entirely, Charlotte Mensah often opts for “man-made fibers that have been designed to perform like human hair and that can still be styled with heat tools” in her salon. This is an area of category expansion, with synthetic products from Outré reaching viral fame and plant-based hair brands like Rebundle, Knott and Ruka Hair attracting new audiences with innovative alternatives to traditional human hair extensions.

Ruka Hair’s latest invention (a plastic-free, collagen-based braiding fiber called Synths 2) became the first-ever hair extension of its kind to land in Sephora doors this summer. Stockist support of this caliber affirms it: the wig business is big business indeed, and with the right sourcing, can set savvy founders up for exponential growth.

Beauty Brands Are Chasing Purity in a Polluted World

Does Beauty Need to Reinvest in Innovation?

Is Your Beauty Brand Actually Ready for Retail?

Meet the Wellness Brands Riding the Anti-Optimization Wave

#6926. Dreaming of Buying a Home Abroad? These 10 Countries Make It Shockingly Simple

If you’ve always wanted to live out your expat fantasies, start here.

Over the last few years, many Americans have daydreamed about life abroad. Moving internationally can often feel like a pipe dream, but with the right planning, it’s possible. However, lengthy visa processes, daunting language barriers, and a difficult-to-navigate legal system can make the dream feel more like a nightmare.

Certain countries, like Australia, Canada, and New Zealand, have bans on foreign buyers. Others, like Thailand, Cuba, and Vietnam, don’t allow outsiders to own property at all. On the other end of the spectrum are countries actively trying to attract foreign buyers, offering enviable incentives like affordable home prices, a smoother customs process, and an easier-to-navigate work-visa approval process.

Choosing the right location can make moving abroad much less intimidating. Ahead, we’re breaking down the ten best countries for Americans looking to buy property abroad. Whether you’re dreaming of relocating or searching for your perfect vacation home, these countries make buying as easy as possible and offer gorgeous landscapes too.

If you’ve ever dreamed of living among medieval architecture and rolling mountain ranges, Georgia might be the key to unlocking your fantasy. A bridge between Eastern Europe and Western Asia, the transcontinental country borders Russia, Turkey, Armenia, and the Black Sea, offering both subtropical and high-altitude climates. Thankfully, the real estate landscape is much less complex, with few legal or bureaucratic hurdles for American buyers, no residency or visa required (for up to a year), and remarkably fast processing times. In fact, the only major restriction in the Georgia market is that foreigners cannot purchase or own agricultural land, a nod to the importance of the local, small-scale farming cultures peppered across the country’s regions.

Uruguay’s buying process could not be more straightforward. South America’s second-smallest sovereign country requires no citizenship, visa, or prior approvals. Under Uruguayan law, foreign buyers are given the same rights as citizens and enjoy the benefits of free capital flow. Locals are just as, if not more, tourist-friendly. The country is known for its hospitality, laid-back café culture, and stunning coastal landscapes.

Instead of retiring to Florida, consider this balmy, beautiful Caribbean country—just a four-hour direct flight from the New York area. Like everything else on the island, the real estate laws are relatively relaxed, making buying a house easy-breezy; and you can buy and own property without obtaining residency status or special permissions, as is the case in many other Caribbean islands. “Foreigners can own property outright in the Dominican Republic, with legal protections for foreign investors,” says Mauricio Umansky, CEO and founder of The Agency. One important consideration is financing: You can certainly get a mortgage in the DR, but it may not be quite as cushy as what you can get in the U.S.—so pay cash if possible.

Great news if you’ve been wanting a more provincial life: Americans face zero restrictions when buying homes in France. The open market allows for statesiders to buy houses, apartments, or plots of land in the city, countryside, and coastal towns in person or remotely without needing citizenship, residency, or even a local visa. However, a purchase does not grant residency, so if you’re hoping to make France your permanent home, you’ll want to apply for a separate long-stay visa and note the Schengen 90-out-of-180-day rule. While buying may be simple, the finances are anything but. Closing fees, including notary fees, typically add seven to ten percent to your purchase. Mortgage rates are also incredibly hard to secure for U.S.-based buyers, thanks to strict debt-to-income caps and FATCA compliance. To simplify the process, many buyers opt to put 20 percent to 40 percent down in cash.

Perhaps the most American-friendly market, Panama boasts a large English-speaking market, virtually no restrictions on foreign property ownership, and uses the U.S. dollar as legal tender in a vastly affordable market. The Central American nation also offers residency programs like the Friendly Nations Visa, clearing the way legally for residency by investment. In addition to an efficient legal system and reliable market, Panama boasts breathtaking beaches, lush rainforests, and exceptional wildlife.

If you’re hoping for a home in the coffee valleys of Colombia, you’ll need to hire a real estate lawyer before you start scouting for mountain chalets, coffee triangle treehouses, and rainforest retreats. The Caribbean country opts out of standardized agent licensing systems, making legal aid essential to verify title and freedom certificates. While citizenship isn’t required, you will need to secure a tourist visa and Colombian Tax ID to complete your purchase and register for property taxes.

The buying process in Greece is lengthy, though it is streamlined, with a clear checklist for American buyers to find success in the market. Hiring a local lawyer or notary is a great first step after scouting properties. Not only are they familiar with title checks and contracts, but they can also navigate the intricacies that come with buying in designated border areas or near military zones, which require special clearance for non–EU buyers. While the most popular areas for American buyers, like Athens and Crete, don’t come with heavy legal intricacies, buying any property in Greece will mean securing a Greek Tax Number—a small price to pay for cities with such rich histories and breathtaking seaside views.

The people in this popular Western European coastal country are famously friendly and inviting, and that extends to real estate. According to Samira Easton, a certified international property specialist with Premier Sotheby’s International Realty, Portugal offers a Golden Visa Program, which makes it simple to buy property and gain residency; clear real estate laws; and a well-established network of English-speaking real estate agents, lawyers, and notaries to help you during the process.

Belize is home to one of the most vibrant, tourist-friendly cultures in Central America, mixing Mayan, Hispanic, Asian, and Creole cultures in its dense rainforests and rolling low plains. Buying here is as easy as it can get, as outright ownership applies to foreigners, and a British-inspired legal system abolishes language barriers and makes contracts easier to understand. Even more, there is no capital gains tax in Belize, and property taxes trend low. The country also boasts vibrant marine life and is home to the world’s second-largest barrier reef.

Spain may have ended its investor visa program in 2025, but the real estate market is still remarkably easy for Americans to navigate. The country currently imposes no restrictions on foreign or nonresident property ownership, but there are a few things you’ll need to procure before you buy, like a Foreigner Identification Number, which is mandatory for taxes. Despite the minor hurdles, the market is booming both on the ground and online. “There are also numerous online resources and property portals available, so it’s easy to find listings and information,” Easton says. This is a boon for those who aren’t able to make the trip over to Europe to house shop in person.Note that buying property does not grant you residency or bypass the country’s 90-day Schengen limit. If you’re hoping to turn your Spanish holiday into your everyday, you’ll have to apply for a separate visa.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6923. From Practical Gifts to Wellness Moments: Holiday Trends to Watch for 2026

As the holidays beckon, a younger, fashion-led contingent of consumers across the US and Europe are upping their spend despite inflation, higher living costs and economic volatility. The situation doesn’t make sense on paper, but on department store floors, the verdict is clear: The holidays are still big business for luxury — they just look a little different.

“[US consumers] are signaling that they’re going to continue to be resilient shoppers and lean into the holiday season,” says Brian McCarthy, Deloitte’s retail strategy leader, pinpointing millennials as the top spenders this year by a long shot — in part because of life stage. “Millennials and Gen Z now have some discretionary spending, and they’re starting to hit the age where they have children,” he adds, advising that department stores focus on these two cohorts.

Across the board, high-net-worth individuals (HNWI) are more discerning than ever — spending more where necessary, for gifts centered on individuality, performance, wellness and sentimentality.

Deloitte’s holiday report predicts that average household holiday spending in the US will increase 4% year-on-year to $1,654 per household in 2026. In Europe, shoppers are expected to spend 3.4% more year-on-year this holiday season, per data from Mastercard. In the US, millennials and Gen Z will make up 50% of spending — a first since the annual study began — with both groups upping their spend at a double-digit rate year-on-year. Most holiday shoppers expect to spend the most money at either mass merchants or online, followed by department stores.

McCarthy highlights some core stats luxury retailers must consider: 72% of Gen Z say they’re interested in purchasing luxury goods as gifts for the holiday season, followed by 61% of millennials, 40% of Gen X, and 29% of baby boomers. The data also reveals that 76% of Gen Z plan on gifting clothing and accessories, up from 67% last year.

The K-shaped economy — where the top end of the economy grows, but the lower end lags as inequalities widen — will ultimately benefit luxury fashion retailers, many of whom rely on the top percentile spenders to turn a profit. Nonetheless, even HNWIs are not immune to the same financial pressures as the aspirational and middle class, confirms Mintel. Analysts agree that price, value, convenience and product will dictate consumption across demographics this holiday period.

Holiday shopping at Harrods is well underway.

“Financial pressures continue to influence holiday spending, creating a resilient but resourceful consumer who is looking for deals and becoming more deliberate about where they spend,” says Brittany Steiger, Mintel’s associate principal for retail and e-commerce, noting that 72% of US luxury shoppers say price alone isn’t an indicator of luxury, and 41% consider discounts important.

The macroeconomic environment is tricky, but it’s also a well-established mood of uncertainty. Per Mintel, 59% of shoppers admit financial stress is making it harder to celebrate in the way they prefer, but 39% are still willing to spend on truly meaningful purchases. Against this backdrop, the smartest retailers have risen to the challenge, aligning their product assortments with shifting consumer trends.

That said, the rollout of Christmas shopping remains largely the same. “We typically see two distinct phases: the first is about preparation, with clients investing in their own wardrobes ahead of festive celebrations,” says Simon Longland, Harrods’s director of fashion buying, pointing to partywear, evening-wear, knitwear and exceptional outerwear. Then, “as Christmas approaches, the focus shifts toward gifting.”

Below, Vogue Business collates this year’s key gifting predictions.

For retailers, occasion dressing continues to drive growth over the holidays, but shoppers are displaying a more playful, casual approach, tracking with a younger consumer skew.

At Bloomingdale’s, ritzy festive occasionwear will be a key driver. “This holiday, we can put the pursuit of the perfect wardrobe on hold for a moment and indulge in the sheer joy of fashion for fashion’s sake,” says fashion director David Thielebeule. “After seasons of restraint, fashion is remembering how to have fun. Rich color, sumptuous textures and a little extravagance are making getting dressed feel exciting again.”

Sebla Refiğ Devidas, Turkish retailer Communité’s chief merchandising officer, reports a similar appetite for eveningwear this holiday period. “In womenswear, we expect party dressing to be a big part of holiday shopping, especially pieces with interesting details that can be worn in different ways,” she says. “There’s a sense of 1920s glamour in the lace, embellishments, fringed silk, and rich textures, reflected in pieces from Conner Ives, Dries Van Noten, Ann Demeulemeester, Simone Rocha, Anna October, Fforme and Enfants Riches Déprimés.”

At End, statement separates and directional designs from Simone Rocha, Cecilie Bahnsen, Rick Owens, Peachy Den and Gimaguas are outperforming traditional partywear. “We’re taking a broader and more fashion-led approach to occasion dressing this holiday season,” says Martin Wieczorek, End’s chief product officer. “We’re also seeing a notable shift toward separates over dresses, with customers seeking greater versatility and long-term wardrobe value, which is reflected in our assortment”

Within menswear, Longland notes that while established party-wear brands and tailoring services will continue to play a significant role for Harrods this holiday season, the store is already seeing clients explore more pioneering attire. “We believe menswear today is as much about individual expression as it is about the traditional codes of dressing,” he says. “The festive season is a particularly interesting moment for this, whether clients are discovering a new designer or investing in beautifully tailored pieces for a special occasion.”

Even among luxury shoppers, practicality is becoming a core tenet of holiday gifting.

“Confidence is low and spending is still up, which tells you people are not buying less, they are buying more carefully,” reports Christophe Roberts, founder and CCO of New York-based concept store Seed. What’s working? “Shoes, outerwear and the good $50 gift. The one big present is getting replaced by two or three smaller [practical] ones that actually say something about you.”

British retailer End reports an uptick in pragmatic premium outerwear gifting from brands including Our Legacy, Stone Island, Salomon, Arc’teryx, Barbour and Damson Madder. “We’re seeing menswear customers gravitate toward two key style directions,” says Wieczorek, pointing to a continuation of outdoor dressing, functional accessories and hiking-influenced footwear. (In this vein, demand for Stone Island knits has grown 36% since October, per Lyst’s 2026 gift index.)

On the other side of menswear gifting, End also reports a hunger for relaxed tailoring and elevated outerwear — “a long tailored coat is a must!” says Wieczorek — from brands such as Studio Nicholson, Auralee and YMC. In keeping with this desire for lasting purchases, Mintel reports that 71% of US men see luxury purchases as a personal milestone or marker of achievement, which makes meaningful wardrobe upgrades and keepsake gifts especially resonant. “We’re seeing strong momentum across jewellery and watches, a category that continues to gain traction during the gifting season as customers seek elevated, personal, enduring purchases,” says Wieczorek.

Calvin Klein underwear remains a strong stocking filler, boosted by the brand’s hit campaign.

In New York-based concept store Essx, utility reigns supreme among menswear shoppers, bolstered by more experimental outerwear. “As the holiday season approaches, we expect to see a continued focus on utility shopping paired with experimental and statement outerwear. Leather has been one of our strongest categories over the past few seasons with VIPs and walk-in clients,” says Jackson Ray, store director. “We anticipate a continued desire for leather pants and jackets paired with sterling silver accessories and leather boots.”

Essx also predicts keepsake pieces to perform. “Our customers always seek exclusive or limited items,” says Ray. “We have heavily invested in upscale accessories such as fine jewelry, eyewear, and footwear, which make for fantastic holiday gifts.

Unsurprisingly, underwear remains a trusty stocking filler for shoppers, especially this time round. Demand for men’s Calvin Klein underwear climbed 60% year-on-year in October 2026 versus October 2025, according to Lyst’s 2026 gift index. Following the Jude Bellingham campaign, searches soared a further 25% week-on-week (Nov 2-9 2026).

Womenswear shoppers are also seeking timeless gifts this holiday season. “For womenswear, quality, versatility and longevity can help justify the splurge,” says Steiger, noting that 76% of US luxury consumers prefer timeless or classic styles, where pieces feel special enough to gift but relevant beyond the holiday season. At Selfridges, HNWIs are showing strong interest in labels such as Phoebe Philo and The Row for this holiday season. “Investment dressing continues to be a key theme, with elevated knitwear emerging as a standout category,” notes Bosse Myhr, director of buying for menswear, womenswear and childrenswear at Selfridges.

Retailers still want to see newness within established brands, though. “This Christmas marks an important moment for womenswear at Harrods, as many of the collections introduced by newly appointed creative directors began arriving in store for SS26,” explains Longland. “These new creative visions are bringing fresh energy to established houses, and we expect the new icons emerging from these collections to feature prominently on Christmas wish lists.” Longland notes that Harrods’s clients are responding to pieces that feel distinctive, rather than plainly familiar.

Tried-and-tested holiday favorites will remain core across genders. “In our stores right now, we’ve launched cashmere shops, scarf bars, trench destinations and gifting at all levels of luxury prices,” said Burberry CEO Joshua Schulman, on the brand’s latest earnings call. Burberry just staged a takeover of New York hotel The Mark to promote its scarves and partywear with the all-important US consumer. Flannels features the Burberry scarf bars in-store, too. “You can’t go wrong with an iconic British scarf for Christmas. It’s one of those gifts that feels special but will be worn for years to come,” says Natalie Dickson, Frasers Group’s head of luxury brand partnerships.

According to PWC, 78% of US Gen Z consumers are seeking nostalgic, screen-free gifts. For US consumers gifting children (17 and under) in their family, clothing and footwear were the second most-cited category (60%), following toys (63%). Each of the top five categories are all analog.

In line with Gen Z and millennial households stepping into parenthood — and therefore becoming core gift-givers — childrenswear is showing more and more affinities with adult tastes. “There’s a growing crossover between adult and childrenswear, with families shopping the designer brands they know and love across the whole family,” reports Dickson.

At Harrods, a similar shift holds, with childrenswear holiday demand led by the same categories driving womenswear and menswear: partywear, pyjamas and outerwear. “Christmas at Harrods is one of those moments when dressing becomes a shared family occasion,” says Longland. “From special outfits for celebrations to beautiful winter pieces, clients are looking for clothing that combines practicality with a sense of occasion.”

One of the most notable areas of growth within childrenswear has been the baby category, which has significantly outperformed the broader children’s market at Selfridges this year, says Myhr.

In the kids category, parents’ own memories of Christmas and holiday style guide product assortments. “There’s a wonderful nostalgia to dressing children for the holidays, and nobody understands that quite like Ralph Lauren,” says Bloomingdale’s Thielebeule, calling out Fair Isle knits, tartan and velvet. For him, these are tropes that “feel as though they belong in today’s family holiday card, but are just as lovely a generation later.”

Last year, Ralph Lauren’s big Christmas push went viral. In response, Bloomingdale’s has invested in a special holiday partnership this year, entitled “A Treasured World”, which offers a whole family range of gifting and cozy seasonal wear, explains Thielebeule. It builds on the viral traction Ralph Lauren demonstrated last year with “Ralph Lauren Mom” creators on TikTok and Instagram presenting themselves as festive, old-money Americans, he adds.

Luxury brands have been ramping up their investments in wellness this holiday season — tapping into a market now valued at $2.7 trillion, according to McKinsey. This has trickled down into holiday offerings. “Wellness is opening new territory for luxury gifting, particularly as consumers increasingly invest in how they feel as well as how they look,” says Mintel’s Steiger, citing Dior’s “Haute Wellness” capsule, launched in September, which comprises a Pilates and yoga mat, a yoga block, a Pilates ring, resistance bands and weighted wristbands.

In self-gifting, 32% of US shoppers cited health and wellness products as tempting — making them the third-highest-scoring category in Deloitte’s survey, after clothing and accessories, and electronics. Meanwhile, 44% of shoppers will gift products from the health and wellness category this year, up from 40% in 2025. Fittingly, one of the top brands in Lyst’s gifting index is Alo, with demand increasing 132% this year.

The rise in wellness gifting aligns with another undercurrent. Per PWC, 81% of US Gen Z consumers are prioritizing offline, in-person activities this year, with 63% saying a shared meal would matter more as a gift than a physical present.

Nonetheless, the bottom line remains the same across gift categories. “This holiday, the opportunity is in products that offer something beyond the price tag, whether that’s emotional significance, lasting quality, personal wellbeing, or simply something worth keeping,” says Steiger.

Update: This story has been updated with Natalie Dickson’s correct title, Frasers Group’s head of luxury brand partnerships.

#6919. All the Standout Fall/Winter 2026 Fashion Campaigns So Far

The fashion world is already offering its first look at what we’ll be coveting this season. The biggest houses are ushering in fall/winter 2026 with campaigns that blend hallmarks of the season with transitional staples, from Celine’s sleek vision lensed by Zoë Ghertner to Fendi’s Peekaboo bag taking center stage on Monica Bellucci and Yara Shahidi to Louis Vuitton’s and Chanel’s après-ski wonderlands.

#6916. Ezra Sosa Called Out These ‘DWTS’ Pros for “Betting Against Him” in a Since-Deleted TikTok

Though Ezra Sosa and his celeb partner, Julia Stiles, were eliminated after performing a paso doble to “Satisfied” from Hamilton on Dancing With the Stars’ Disney Night, the pro is still keeping up with the competition—and he seemingly called out some shady behind-the-scenes behavior from former DWTS pros.

After Tuesday night’s shocking results, Ezra posted—and later deleted—a TikTok where he wrote, “I wonder how much money some former pros have lost on Kalshi…”

Ezra seemingly slammed Maks Chmerkovskiy and Gleb Savchenko for placing bets against their fellow pros on Kalshi.

“get them ezra, that behavior is so weird from former pros, they are such losers,” one fan wrote after a screenshot of his TikTok circulated on Reddit.

Another pointed out that other former pros, including Sasha Farber and Peta Murgatroyd, have also placed online bets throughout season 35.

“I hope that all the former pros betting on the show never get asked back in any capacity, even in the live audience,” they wrote. “It’s a massive conflict of interest and can actually sway voting patterns if Kalshi bets on dwts become more widespread and normalized. Maks, Gleb, Sasha, Peta I’m looking at you losers!”

However, someone chimed in with a valid point: “Unfortunately they’re not losing their own money (in fact they’re actually getting paid), they’re just trying to influence their followers to lose money on what should be an illegal app.”

Ezra isn’t the first person to call former pros out on their bets, either.

During a November 18 interview with USA Today, Jan Ravnik made a pointed remark when asked to leave a message for his haters: “I hope you lose all your money.”

The day before that, Jan made a dig at Maks during a DWTS livestream when Brandon Armstrong and Alan Bersten commended him for his “beautiful” choreography. “Say that to Maks,” he said.

This all comes after Maks and Gleb publicly shared their DWTS season 35 bets online, but Maks has long criticized Jan’s involvement on the show.

During an October 2025 episode of The Penthouse with Peta podcast, Maks claimed, “I’m sorry, Jan has absolutely no business being a pro on Dancing with the Stars. There’s zero foundation, technique, quality, understanding of the partnership.”

He has since apologized to Jan, but still placed bets against him so…who even knows what’s going on.

#6914. Is Merch Stealing Luxury’s Aspirational Consumer?

Typically, middle-income young shoppers have focused their fashion spending on accessible luxury goods or one-off splurges, like a Coach Tabby bag ($375) or a Longchamp Le Pliage ($165).

However, among these groups, a growing faction is trading down, looking instead to the fast-developing category of merch to broadcast their affiliation to culture, whether it’s the newly launched Rhode windbreaker ($257) or last week’s frenzied release of a hot-pink GTA logo tee ($66). While merch is nothing new in the fashion market — Balenciaga relied heavily on it during Demna’s tenure between 2015 and 2025 — the playing field is changing, with a stronger focus on the temporal buzz of a new digital or game-based craze, the cult movie everyone’s watching, the fiendishly anticipated alt-rap tour, or a new milestone in a girl-boss beauty community’s trajectory — like Rhode founder Hailey Bieber’s 30th birthday.

“Brands invest inordinate amounts of resources into product research and development to try and catch the ever-elusive hype cycle. But with [consumers] suddenly coveting more transient ‘merchandise’ over main line product ranges, is that investment misplaced?” asks Simon Richardson, group strategy director at creative agency Amplify. “What [brands] should be doing is creating a moment in time, not just a product line. Fans need more — all the time — to signal what they care about in a way that feels distinct, real and truly of a moment.”

Recently, Jordan Firstman, director of buzzy gay drama Club Kid, called on New York designer Emily Dawn Long — renowned among Dimes Square it-kids for her in-the-know screenprinted vintage tees — to design the merch for the film. The designer, known for her Spoiled Boys slogan tee, emblazoned the ragged tops with one of the film’s lines, “It’s like that every time”. In the film itself, Firstman also wore his own Emily Dawn Long tee, printed with the words, “Take off your clothes”. On Wednesday, A24 took over a downtown 1<3 NY souvenir shop to sell the capsule, before releasing a limited online drop (most styles are sold out).

A24 worked with designer Emily Dawn Long for new film Club Kid’s merch.

Tech bros are also tapping in. This year, Trump-contracted data surveillance firm Palantir released a chore jacket ($239) not dissimilar to those purveyed by cult restaurants, such as Sessions Arts Club and St. John. Reportedly, the company’s broader apparel line is performing, especially in South Korea, where the windbreaker ($170) sold out. Much-talked-about corporation Nvidia — whose CEO Jensen Huang stepped down this week following his extramarital affair with a potential Chinese spy — also released a sweater emblazoned with a leather-jacketed cartoon of Huang ($178). The design is picking up strong resale value again following the scandal: one seller listed the design on Ebay for $2,500. And OpenAI offers a racing-style shirt ($75) not dissimilar to styles from Corteiz, Palace and Supreme — as well as bumper stickers ($10) and caps ($40), all sold on its website.

As luxury struggles to justify its price hikes — since the average price of personal luxury goods increased by 52% between 2019 and 2024, per HSBC — the merch market has swooped in to capitalize on the aspirational shopper. In the US, consumer sentiment is low, and yet spending is up. Indeed, across the US, Europe and China, shoppers are more cautious and discerning with discretionary purchases, guided by a brand’s value propositions. If a band tee, a Roblox hoodie, or a pair of GTA x Travis Scott Nike Air Force 1 sneakers can communicate cultural belonging, community adjacency, and lifestyle for a fraction of the cost of a luxury handbag, then merch wins.

Indeed, in late 2025, 72% of Gen Z luxury shoppers told Vogue Business they would prefer owning a Walmart “Wirkin” over a genuine Hermès Birkin, and when interviewed, several respondents said they deem spending lots of money on luxury distasteful and that luxury goods are not worth the cost. At the same time, beauty continues to boom with aspirational shoppers, but — unlike clothing and accessories — has only a handful of ways to turn its goods into walking advertisements. The Rhode phone case, seen in influencer selfies, marked the first step towards a solution, then came the wearable merch. Similarly, tech giants largely deal in intangible goods. But physical merch is a way for fans to display their favorite chip-maker or AI platform.

Hailey Bieber wears her new Rhode windbreaker, which retails at $257.

For beauty, and even tech, merch gives brand fans a way to express their appreciation of the products and the communities around them. Companies recognize young people’s appetite to feel part of something.

Millennial readers will remember when “merch” referred primarily to band T-shirts. That playbook began in musical subcultures and has now transferred to a broader market. “Gen Alpha grew up inside worlds. For them, a ‘skin’ [Fortnite’s in-game outfits] is merch, a creator’s snack bar is merch, and a hoodie is the physical version of a world they already spend hours in,” says Jolyon Varley, co-founder of marketing agency OK Cool.

“Logos used to confer status and were a more reliable indicator of what you could afford or how you were living,” he adds. “Merch tells people what you’re into, and that’s become far more valuable. It’s a matter of signaling taste rather than wealth.”

Varley points to the frenzy seen at GTA’s Selfridges launch, explaining that consumers now associate with much more than simply products, but instead entire worlds. “GTA isn’t just a game any more,” he says. “Millions of people live in it. The launch week proved it.”

Whether that’s an event exclusive, an IP artefact made real for the ‘heads’, or even a staff uniform, exclusivity and experience are major drivers of hype, says Amplify’s Richardson. “Something that says, ‘I was there’, not just ‘this is what I’m into’.”

Richardson also notes that fandom thrives on shared codes and signals. So his advice for brands wanting a piece of the pie? “Bringing together your own lore, something that anchors your merch in time, and the right collaborators to bring it to life in a fan-first and agile way, can turn a small run of products into a mass-reach driver.” The Rockstar Games’s GTA takeover of Miami — a reported $3.9 million deal with Miami-Dade County — sets the bar, lighting up Miami Heat’s stadium, dressing players in black-and-pink jerseys, and activating across the city.

Merch’s appeal lies in the power of the brand and what that communicates. It’s a quick win to cross categories, too. “If you build a brand that is cool, interesting, culturally relevant, aspirational, it [almost] doesn’t matter what you’re selling, because what you’re selling is the aura — this ineffable, ‘it’, cool factor,” says Ursula founder and brand strategist Emma Hope Allwood. “Just practically, when you have these big cultural moments or launches like GTA, it makes sense to try to break it down into different categories — different ways for people to be part of it, rather than it just being a game.”

Luxury brands that want to re-enter the merch market and cultural players looking to seize the growing momentum need to broach the sector carefully. “Not all merch is cool,” says Sara McAlpine, co-founder of brand and marketing studio Bikini, highlighting the recent tech brand plays. “There’s no reason to be producing a chore coat unless you’re trying to say you’re aligned with a Carhartt WIP and Japanese-denim wearing aesthetic-conscious crowd.”

So what works? Influencer and DJ Leo Mandella visited the GTA Selfridges pop-up. He typically doesn’t consider merch cool — “[it] can look a bit gimmicky” — but the capsule delivered, he believes. “It’s very easy for gaming companies to mess up on the rest of the promo run, but Rockstar were absolutely incredible at creating a capsule of merch that was just as good as the game,” says Mandella, who spent £250 (roughly $329) at the pop-up. “I think merch is especially exciting when done well because you feel part of a moment. It’s not just a piece of clothing. Buying a Vice City tee or a Burger Shot uniform [the official in-game restaurant chain] makes you feel like you’re in this fictional world.”

Nonetheless, for brands that still want to communicate esotericism, merch remains an astute method. Varley pinpoints the power of releasing one-off, friends-and-family T-shirts during events. He cites the Awake x Jordan T-shirts — art directed by London graffiti artist Fatzoo — given out during Notting Carnival at the Deviation stage. “That immediately orients you in a specific nucleus of that scene,” he says. “If you weren’t there — or don’t know the individuals involved — you didn’t get one.”

#6908. Katie Holmes’s Holiday Party Trick? Matching Her Lipstick to Her Bag

A flash of red is a holiday party classic, and Katie Holmes is making a persuasive case for doubling down on the festive color. The actor stepped out in New York for a holiday celebration hosted by Meiomi Pinot Noir, with her boyfriend Jason Bard Yarmosky by her side, serving up a masterclass in laidback party dressing.

While fellow guests embraced the season’s faux fur, feathers, and sequins, Holmes stuck to her trusty winter uniform: a sweeping black coat from Lafayette 148 layered over a simple white top and dark Banana Republic corduroys, with black shoes by The Row. The pièce de résistance was a cherry-red Gucci shoulder bag, perfectly coordinated with her punchy red lipstick.

It’s an easy styling trick, but an effective one. The two flashes of crimson brought a festive sensibility to an otherwise pared-back ensemble, while the lipstick also felt particularly appropriate for an evening celebrating Pinot Noir. Holmes looked very much in the holiday spirit, without a sequin in sight.

Of course, Katie Holmes is something of an It-bag enthusiast: a classic quilted Chanel 19 Flap Bag, the sleek Private Eye bag by Métier, the trapezoid Akris Ai shoulder bag, an iconic Celine Triomphe, a suite of Ferragamo. Her extensive rotation runs from roomy everyday totes to polished, structured shoulder bags, often serving as the focal point of her otherwise uncomplicated outfits. She has a knack for making even the most coveted accessories look effortless, pairing them with straight-leg denim jeans and louche tailoring.

And then there’s her enduring affection for a red lip. Holmes has long understood its transformative powers, particularly when paired with her favored minimal makeup and swept-back hair. Here, with her dark hair neatly pulled back, the bold lipstick was allowed to do the talking.

Matchy-matchy glam has been all over the celebrity circuit: everyone from Taylor Swift to Kylie Jenner is choosing coordinating mani-pedis in deep shades of seasonal onyx and Bordeaux, while Jennifer Lopez embraced all-over burgundy.

Forget matching your bag to your shoes. This holiday season, Katie Holmes proves matching it to your makeup can be a true festive delight. And, as demonstrated here, you don’t have to sacrifice comfort—or your favorite corduroys—to look party-ready.

#6904. Which Christmas Tree Is Right for Your Home? Experts Break It Down

Consider this your shortcut to finding the perfect holiday centerpiece.

Holiday decorating is supposed to be a joyous event, but picking the right tree can be surprisingly complicated. There’s a lot of height, circumference, and branch density variation—some people like a full, fluffy look, while others opt for a more minimal tree—and don’t even entice us with the real versus faux debate.

The truth is, any tree can be beautiful in the right setting, so if you’re feeling unsure about which one fits your home, we’ve created the ultimate guide on which tree is right for your holiday centerpiece. Ahead, we're sharing the most popular types of Christmas trees for every household.

The Fraser fir is one of the most popular types of Christmas tree, especially for households east of the Rocky Mountains. Firs, in general, are some of the most common trees grown for the holiday season, says Ward Dilmore, founder of Petrus Landscaping in California. There's a good reason for this: They have a perfect balance of deep green color and even, stiff branching, he says, which makes them a breeze to decorate.

Canaan fir trees often get compared to Fraser and balsam firs, thanks to their similar growing patterns and branch density, but this variety has a major benefit above the rest: needle retention. It sheds less than other firs, which means less cleanup for you.

Often a home for many birds, including woodpeckers, the eastern white pine is fluffy and fast-growing. With foliage that's thin, delicate, and silvery-green, this tree is best paired with lightweight ornaments and minimal decorations.

A classic type of Christmas tree, the noble fir is native to the Cascade and Pacific Coast mountain ranges of the Northwestern United States. With strong branches, this tree ensures your precious family heirlooms won't be at risk of falling, and the long peak is ideal for an oversized tree topper.

The Arizona cypress is the most drought-tolerant evergreen out there—with the right care, this type of Christmas tree can last longer than other evergreens. With its thick, layered branches and lacy silver-blue foliage, this tree is a beautiful alternative to the silver fir.

You’ll find this bushy type of Christmas tree primarily in Southern states, according to the Real Christmas Tree Board. Thanks to its stout branches, it should have no problem holding up your heaviest ceramic ornaments. It also has a piney fragrance and keeps its needles well throughout the season, as long as you give it plenty of water to drink.

Also known as a red fir, these striking trees are one of the rarest types of Christmas trees, says horticulturist and botanical designer Nathan Heinrich. They're slow-growing, and only a few farmers and harvesters have permits in California and Oregon to harvest them in the wild each year, he explains. "The reason these trees make such perfect Christmas trees is because they have such perfectly spaced, open, elegant branches that make hanging ornaments and lights easy," Heinrich says.

Like Miracle on 34th Street and peppermint hot chocolate, the balsam fir is a holiday classic. It's also the most fragrant of all types of Christmas trees, according to the Real Christmas Tree Board. You can find these at Christmas tree farms in Canada, New England, the upper Great Lakes, and Pennsylvania, along with lots of retail locations east of the Rocky Mountains.

The Scotch pine often flies under the radar, but it's a designer-favorite Christmas tree choice, and it has great needle retention. "With vibrant green and durable needles that resist shedding, it's low maintenance," says New York designer Artem Kropovinsky of interior design studio Arsight. "Its robust branches accommodate even the heaviest ornaments, and its dense appearance gives rooms a fuller, festive look."

The trees you see in lots often have been pruned and sheared into the quintessential Christmas tree shape, explains Lisa Tadewaldt, an ISA-certified arborist and owner of Portland, Oregon–based Urban Forest Professionals. She recommends getting a permit and cutting a tree that's native to your area down in the forest. In the Pacific Northwest, her favorite tree in its natural form is a grand fir. "It is going to give more Charlie Brown vibes than Hallmark Christmas vibes, but it has a flat needle pattern that gives it an air of elegance that balances out the thinner, more homespun branching pattern," she says.

With its signature bluish needles, the robust blue spruce has a full, triangular shape, says Kropovinsky. "Apart from its signature color, this tree releases a fresh scent similar to frosty woodlands," he says. Bonus for pet owners: "Given the tree's pointed foliage, pets often steer clear of it."

With its flexible needles and twigs, the Eastern red cedar doesn't hold ornaments all that well. But don’t let that deter you! If your heart is set on this hardy juniper, it looks great draped with lights, says ISA-certified master arborist Blake Watkins, operations partner at Monster Tree Service.

The Douglas fir is a type of Christmas tree that is synonymous with the Pacific Northwest, but is available at holiday tree markets nationwide. It's also one of the more affordable types of Christmas trees you can find. "Dark green foliage and hanging needles make this tree gorgeous even without ornaments," Watkins says.

The concolor fir (also called the white fir) is the type of tree you'd like to sidle up next to with a mug of mulled wine. While many trees have pine scents, this one actually gives off a citrus aroma that has garnered it some devoted fans, according to the Michigan State University Extension. Looks-wise, its branches have long, silvery, bluish-green needles.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

Meghan Shouse is House Beautiful's Digital Editor, and she's been with the team since September 2023. On top of being the magazine's go-to Waco expert, Meghan also touches on quite a few other verticals in the digital space, like hot news content featuring your favorite HGTV stars, home design inspiration, and the latest interior TikTok trends. However, her favorite pieces are the ones that start conversations, such as stories about resale value and whether or not the Solo cup is chic. Before becoming a home writer, Meghan worked in the fashion industry for two years, writing for Harper's Bazaar after graduating from Iowa State University with a bachelor's degree in apparel merchandising. When she's not interviewing interior designers about home trends, you can find Meghan reading a fictional thriller with her cat in her lap, planning themed parties, or strolling through flea markets searching for chunky candle holders and vintage bookends.

Blake Watkins is an ISA board-certified Master Arborist and an ASCA-registered Consulting Arborist located in Sacramento, CA. He is the National Director of Plant Health Care for Monster Tree Service and is the owner of Arborist Blake Watkins LLC.

#6900. Chanel Chance Eau Splendide Is My Favorite of All the Chance Perfumes. Here’s Why I Love It So Much

Allow me to make the case for wearing it year-round.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

My mom’s Chanel No.5, a fixture in our bathroom cabinet, was the first perfume I ever knew by name. It’s sophisticated and timeless, much like most of the other best-of-the-best Chanel fragrances that have been released since No.5’s 1921 debut. And while I have nothing but respect for the classics, I’ll admit that some of the brand’s more traditional floral fragrances aren’t necessarily what I reach for on a regular basis. I’m usually more interested in something a little fruitier, fresher, and more playful.

Enter Chanel Chance Eau Splendide, the purple-hued 2025 addition to the brand’s beloved Chance fragrance collection. With a sparkling raspberry opening, a heart of rose geranium, and a soft, musky base, it promises a bright, youthful take on the classic Chanel scent profile. And as someone who spends a considerable amount of time sniffing new perfumes for work, I know things are serious when they earn an immediate permanent spot on my vanity.

After attending the launch event for Eau Splendide last April, I didn’t intend to make it my new go-to... but that’s exactly what happened. I got an early sample, started wearing it, and simply noticed that I was reaching for it nearly everyday. I quietly fell in love with it as it sneakily became one of my signature scents. Now, after wearing it pretty consistently for a year and a half, here’s my honest review of Chanel Chance Eau Splendide Eau de Parfum, including what it actually smells like on skin, how it wears throughout the day, and whether it’s worth the luxury price tag. Ahead, everything to know before adding it to your fragrance collection.

First things first: Chanel Chance Eau Splendide is a fruity floral, with a much brighter opening than you might expect from a traditional Chanel perfume. I’m not exactly surprised that I love it so much, given that fruity florals make up about 90 percent of my perfume collection.

The fragrance features a raspberry top note that gives it a juicy, slightly tart fruity sweetness. But rather than leaning into the saccharine, sugary territory of some fruity perfumes, Eau Splendide then introduces rose geranium as a heart note, which adds a fresh, rosy, and surprising-but-subtle minty moment. To me, this is how you construct a perfect fruity floral: Before it gets too sweet (and for me, headache-y), some fresh floral notes balance everything out.

But that’s not all: As the fragrance dries down, you’ll experience base notes of iris, cedar, and white musk that make for a soft finish. To me, it results in a scent that feels elegant, feminine, and sophisticated—very Chanel—without being particularly heavy.

Splendide is a fresh and light scent, but don’t think that means it’s not staying put on your skin throughout the day. It usually lasts about four to six hours on me, so while it’s certainly not the longest-lasting fragrance I own, about four spritzes of it gets me through most of the day.

With so many great Chanel fragrances, how does this stand up against the rest? If I had to describe the overall vibe, I’d say that Chance Eau Splendide makes me feel effortlessly put together, but it also has a playful streak to it. I smell the raspberry most prominently on my skin, though, as it gets later in the day, I also notice the iris and musk. Even though I have dozens of fruity floral perfumes, it’s different than any of the others in my fragrance wardrobe.

The first time I spritzed it, I did wonder if it was going to be too sweet for me. Ultimately, I found that the raspberry and rose geranium mix well together and don’t feel too sugary whatsoever. I also love how youthful it is, especially compared to some of the other Chanel fragrances out there. It’s super flirty, vibrant, and fresh, making it the perfect companion for daytime meetings, date nights, and more—I even wore it to Coachella this year. All in all, the one word I’d use to describe it is approachable. Is it the most complex perfume I’ve ever sniffed? No. But the scent, the vibe, and the longevity make it perfect for anyone from a first-time Chanel fragrance wearer (I think it’s a great introduction to the brand) to a fruity floral fanatic. For me, it’s an everyday bottle that takes me from day to night all year long.

On that last point: I can see why someone might call it a spring or summer perfume, given the notes. But there’s no rule that says you have to put away your fruity florals when temperatures drop. This is the kind of fragrance that works whenever I’m craving something bright and fresh.

Okay, so is Chanel Chance Eau Splendide actually worth the splurge? It’ll definitely be a re-purchase for me, and not just because it comes in a pretty purple bottle.

Here’s my case for giving it a chance—yes, pun intended. The juicy raspberry opening is playful without veering into candy territory, while the other notes give it that effortlessly polished Chanel feel. It’s the kind of fruity floral that doesn’t need to be retired the second sweater weather arrives. I say we let this one live its best life all 12 months of the year.

Of course, Chanel prices are Chanel prices (even the smallest bottle is $115), so I’d recommend giving it a test spray before committing to a full bottle—especially if you typically gravitate toward super-sweet gourmands or bold, spicy fragrances. But that being said, I’ve been wearing it regularly for well over a year and I’m nowhere near the end of my bottle, so it’ll last you a while. For anyone who loves a fresh, feminine scent with a little personality, Eau Splendide is definitely worth a sniff. That said, since it’s such an easy-to-wear, clean-meets-powdery-meets-sweet scent, I’d consider it a pretty safe blind buy if that sounds like the type of perfume you generally gravitate toward.

I’ve worn this to weddings, concerts, dinners... You name it, you can wear this perfume there. She’s effortlessly versatile, isn’t overpowering, and isn’t a snob—even though she’s polished, there’s no intimidation factor, and she doesn’t take herself too seriously. She’s a must-have for anyone, whether you’re newly dabbling in fragrance or someone who already swears by a classic fruity floral. Consider this review my formal petition to add it to a list of all-time best year-round scents.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo who covers all things beauty, including fragrance. For this story, she shared her experience wearing the Chanel Chance Eau Splendide Eau de Parfum, one of her go-to perfumes for year-round freshness.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

#6896. Why Am I Still Mad at My Partner After Makeup Sex?

There are some things even an orgasm can’t fix.

Arguments with your partner typically follow a general sequence of events: Something upsets you. You bring it up to your partner. You discuss (or argue). You go back and forth. You eventually work it out. You have wonderful makeup sex. And then—if the argument wasn’t super serious and you were able to hear each other out—you move on (give or take a few “I find it funny how...” monologues and a venting Facetime session with your best friend).

But what if after your conversation and orgasm, you find yourself still upset with your partner? What if, even after putting the argument in the “done” pile, you feel lingering emotions of pain and anger?

While I’m a firm believer that makeup sex is the best stamp of completion for every argument, this isn’t always the case for every relationship. Sometimes you don’t realize that the argument hurt you deeper than you realized—and even a hot and heavy session can’t mask it.

“While some couples experience sex as a bonding and often reparative experience, it may not solve the underlying process of disconnection or conflict in a relationship,” licensed marriage and family therapist Molly Mindel tells Cosmopolitan.

She explains that while sex can be an incredibly powerful tool for connectedness and regulation, it can’t repair bigger issues that may be at play—no matter how good the sex is.

You typically only have makeup sex... when you think you’re making up with your partner. And for many of us, we genuinely believe that’s the case when it happens. But sometimes, we get so caught up in the emotions and connectedness of the sex that we realize we actually don’t feel fully moved on from the argument.

Mandel explains that there are both physiological and psychological reasons sex can make us feel temporarily closer to our partner and less upset about whatever we were arguing about. .

“For one, sex temporarily changes our neurochemical and emotional states,” she says. She explains that oxytocin and dopamine are released during sex, which can make us momentarily feel really good and satisfied. But these chemicals don’t change our foundational relationship beliefs, unmet needs, or feelings about an argument.

“Sex can also regulate ‘attachment distress,’ meaning we get very close and feel very secure temporarily to our partner (maybe even in ways we don't with them outside of a sexual interaction),” she explains. “But after sex is finished and we get back to our relationship, that sense of feeling understood or cared for or connected to can fade, which can cause emotional distress and alter our behavior in the relationship, bringing us right back to our regular cycle of conflict.”

Think of post-nut clarity. While we get swept up in our horniness, makeup sex is simply a band-aid that will typically fall off once the sex is over. And even mind-blowing sex is not enough to keep the illusion going.

It’s not just about the makeup sex. The makeup sex represents the finishing of an argument. And because you already talked about it (and f*cked about it) you should be completely over it, right?

Well, not necessarily. You shouldn’t feel guilty about having unresolved feelings after an argument, especially if you didn’t feel like all of your concerns were addressed.

“Lingering anger, resentment, and hurt days or weeks after an argument with your partner can be a normal part of conflict, and aren't always things we need to rush to ‘solve,’” Mandel says.

Mandel explains that it’s common for people to want to leave conflicts with a sense of resolve—“almost as if we tied a nice bow on the conversation.” But our arguments, like our relationships, are never perfect. It’s less important that an argument is resolved in a certain time period and more that both you and your partner feel heard, respected, and valued—no matter how long it takes.

So if you feel mad even post-makeup sex, that’s totally normal.

“It's worth reflecting on whether the lingering hurt is discomfort from the argument and disconnection, or if there's genuine lack of resolve around a serious issue or emotion needing attention,” Mandel suggests.

If the argument represents a larger pattern in the relationship, this may be the reason you still feel distress even after you “talked it out.” You may have discussed the specific instance, but still need to address the bigger issue at hand. Even if the argument was about something singular, sometimes you just feel like you haven’t fully said your piece.

”In relationships, conflicts are messy, complicated, and cannot be solved in a matter of moments,” Mandel says. “Usually, there is something left unsaid, a feeling that was left unaddressed, or one that comes up later, which can be uncomfortable and sometimes painful.”

If your makeup sex didn’t seem to tie the argument together, you may be worried that nothing will. Don’t panic. Just because an argument leaves some lasting discomfort doesn’t mean that your relationship is doomed—it just means that you need to find a different method to approach the disagreement.

“Sometimes the powerful and healthy thing to do is to say, ‘We may not resolve this entirely’ and ‘Can we learn to be okay with that open ended-ness together for now?’”

And from there, you have to work with your partner to figure out how to move forward. Mandel suggests revisiting the conversation when both partners are calm and emotionally available. This way, she says, both parties will be able to approach the issue with curiosity rather than blame.

Like any argument, it’s going to take a lot of communication and then checking in after the fact. Just keep reminding yourself that there actually is no linear path to resolving conflict. And if both people prioritize each other during a discussion, there’s almost always a way through—even if it does take a few more conversations than expected.

The good news, though? Once the argument really is resolved, and you leave feeling heard and attended to, there’s always more makeup sex to look forward to.

The kitchen has evolved from a hardworking cook space into a multifunctional room for lounging, gathering, and entertaining. So naturally, appliances are changing, too: They need to be as beautiful as they are functional.

If you’re looking to elevate your kitchen, take a cue from our Whole Home. This design is bold enough to impress the design-obsessed and practical enough for the bustle of everyday life.
