#6890. The Horse Girl Trend Is Here for Your Jewelry Box

Horseshoes and tail motifs are dominating charms and rings.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Saddles, stirrups, and horseshoes, oh my: We’ve been firmly in the year of the Fire Horse since February 2026, and the jewelry scene is officially in its horse girl era. Aside from the look being a clear classic in the world of jewelry (horseshoes were popular in Victorian-era jewels), today’s examples vary from sparkly creations to more minimal, toned-down pieces with everyday appeal. No matter the aesthetic, the jewelry of the Year of the Fire Horse is strong: This era only comes around every 60 years in the Chinese zodiac and is said to represent strength, independence, and fearlessness.

Celebrities are clearly fans: Taylor Swift just wore two double-layered horseshoe pendants to announce her Grammy nomination. Horse-ified jewelry also spanned the runway. For spring/summer 2027, both Gucci and Celine revisited the archival horsebit hardware detail and reworked it in a contemporary way to highlight the beautiful lines and sculptural symmetry of the timeless motif. Elsewhere, Chloé’s statement belt covered in huge running horses has been a fashion girl favorite and is the equivalent of jewelry for your waist.

There is perhaps nothing more classic than a chunky horseshoe ring or a horse-themed charm. Verragio and Oscar Heyman each reworked the symbol in gemstone-studded bracelets and double-motif rings. Elsewhere, Lionheart makes a slew of gloriously stunning oversize horse-head pendants and brooches brimming with diamonds and, sometimes, accessorized with mother-of-pearl saddles. The brand also partners directly with 13 Hands Equine Rescue, a nonprofit sanctuary located in Clinton Corners, New York.

But this year, equestrian jewelry is not horsing around when it comes to experimenting. One such example is Hermès’s latest collection, which has an obvious horse connection: saddles, diamond-decked horse heads, and golden saddles. Titled “Into the Horsescape,” the 90-piece lineup is quite literally the horse girl’s dream: lassos in the form of massive baguette-cut diamonds and glimmering oversize horsebit bracelets replete with pavé brown and white diamonds. Pomellato also showcased a carved horse cameo surrounded by brown diamonds and suspended from a delicate rose-gold Dressage-link chain.

If you want to lean into your inner horse girl in a more subtle, everyday way, Gucci’s iconic horsebit rings, refined drop earrings, and sleek bangles offer a sophisticated and versatile place to start your collection. Going vintage is another option: Equestrian themes have been reinterpreted in the jewelry space for hundreds of years now, so there’s ample opportunity. One thing is clear: Horse girl jewelry is galloping from the archives to the runway and into everyday style.

#6887. Mckenna Grace Is Winning the Method Dressing Hunger Games

We spoke to her stylist, Molly Dickson, about creating Mckenna's killer carpet looks for the Sunrise on the Reaping press tour.

Method dressing for press tours is part of the reason 2026’s year of movies feels so unstoppable. The carpets for the latest Hunger Games film, Sunrise on the Reaping, have been heating up look after look, but no one is catching fire quite like Mckenna Grace. Along with her stylist, Molly Dickson, the pair have been turning out absolute masterpieces, with extraordinary commitment to Hunger Games literary lore: As small as ladybugs in Mckenna’s hair (a nod to the ‘Ladybug, Ladybug’ song Haymitch Abernathy sings to Maysilee Donner in Sunrise on the Reaping) to step out in NYC, and as large as a recreation of Katniss’s truly iconic wedding dress from The Hunger Games 2: Catching Fire.

“Mckenna was absolutely certain from the beginning that she wanted to embrace method dressing for this press tour, and I was completely on board,” Molly tells Cosmopolitan.

The concept of method dressing isn’t new, but it’s been incredibly impactful on movie press runs in particular over the past few years. In practice, actors wear looks that reference niche details of the films they’re promoting. Think Zendaya’s Greek-goddess-forward looks for The Odyssey, Margot Robbie’s Barbie ensembles pulled straight from the dolls themselves, Ariana Grande and Cynthia Erivo in a lifetime’s worth of pink and green for Wicked.

“I think the best method dressing moments are the ones that tell a story, while still feeling like incredible fashion looks of their own,” says Molly. “We wanted the fans to have those moments of recognition and excitement, but we also wanted someone who knew nothing about The Hunger Games to look at Mckenna and simply think she looked incredible!”

So how did they do it? What references were on their mood board? What dress almost never saw the light of day? And most importantly: What’s the story behind THE legendary arena dress from the UK premiere? Superstar stylist Molly Dickson answers our most pressing press tour related questions.

It was a really collaborative process between the two of us. Mckenna would come to me with ideas, and I would figure out how to translate them into fashion. She was the one who said, ‘Ladybugs are part of the movie, we need to do a ladybug look!’ From there, I started sourcing pieces that either incorporated ladybugs directly or finding more unexpected, interesting ways to reference them.

We were very intentional about making sure every look had a connection to some part of the Hunger Games universe. We wanted the references to feel thoughtful and recognizable to the fans, while still creating beautiful fashion moments that felt authentic to Mckenna and her personal style.

I don't think either of us initially realized just how invested the Hunger Games fans would be in all the little references and Easter eggs! Once we started seeing that reaction, it became more fun to dig deeper into the franchise and pull inspiration from different characters and iconic moments. Mckenna knows this world so well, and I loved being able to bring her ideas to life.

Mckenna really wanted to pay homage to the strong female characters throughout the franchise—specifically Katniss and Joanna Mason—which became the inspiration for so many of our looks. As we started fittings, we found ways to incorporate inspiration from Rue as well.

We wanted a mix of more recognizable references and subtle nods rather than exact recreations of costumes from the movies. We used the original costumes and characters as our starting point, but then put our own fashion interpretation on them. I think that's where my creativity really comes in. You want fans to recognize the inspiration, but you also want the looks to feel fresh, elevated, and true to the person wearing them.

I really can't think of a better way to generate excitement and conversation around a movie through fashion than method dressing. It's such a fun way to bring the story beyond the screen, and give fans something deeper to connect with. What I loved most about this tour was seeing the conversations happening online, not just about the fashion itself, but about the characters, the references, and ultimately the film.

I think my personal favorite was Mckenna’s Paris look inspired by Katniss's wedding gown. It was such a major fashion moment—we actually went back and forth on whether it might be too much! Mckenna’s team really encouraged us to go for it, and they were absolutely right.

The most challenging part was figuring out how to recreate the sculptural silver elements. Michael Ngo made experimental custom silver pieces for us, which we paired with a beautiful vintage Monique Lhuillier wedding gown. I loved that the complete look felt like a true tribute to Katniss's iconic gown, but still had its own fashion identity. It was one of those moments where the creativity, collaboration, and a little bit of risk-taking really paid off!

We really wanted to pay homage to the strong female characters throughout the Hunger Games franchise while still staying true to Mckenna's personal style. We wanted each look to tell a story and have a connection to the films, but never feel like she was wearing a costume.

For the press junkets and more casual appearances, we focused on subtle nods to the movies, whether that was through color, texture, or small details that fans might recognize. For the bigger red carpet moments, we had the opportunity to be a little more dramatic and lean into those bigger, more established references. The fun of this tour was finding that balance between fashion and storytelling!

Honestly, I have to give all the credit to Mckenna’s mom for this one! She actually discovered the dress on Instagram and sent it over to me. The brand is ALmée, and they’re based in Vietnam.

I love that this look came together in such an unexpected way. It wasn't something we had custom-designed or planned months in advance. It was really just Mckenna's mom spotting something incredible and all of us realizing how perfectly it fit into the Hunger Games world.

That's one of my favorite things about styling: Sometimes the most exciting looks come from the most unexpected places, and this was definitely one of those moments!

One of the looks I was most excited about, but also the most stumped on creatively, Katniss's iconic Girl on Fire gown. The challenge was figuring out how to translate that moment into something that felt like a beautiful red carpet gown rather than a costume.

Cong Tri immediately came to mind because of their intricate beadwork and craftsmanship. I knew I wanted the fire gown to incorporate beading because of the way it catches the light, especially under red carpet lighting and camera flashes. I felt that was going to be key to creating the illusion of flames and bringing the dress to life.

It was honestly the perfect collaboration. The Cong Tri team was so willing to push the design creatively. After experimenting with different fabrics, textures, and techniques, the end result was exactly what we had hoped for. The dress felt dramatic and powerful, with that obvious nod to Katniss, but still felt like a really beautiful fashion moment. That's always the goal with these kinds of looks!

Ali Royals loves true stories and hates writing in the third person, even though she’s doing it right now. Ali is a poet, writer, and denier of any Carrie Bradshaw allegations. Follow her on Instagram and Substack for her daily Outfit Prompts, where she tells herself stories in order to get dressed.

#6884. After Dating Brothers on ‘SLOMW,’ ‘The Circle’ Stars Layla Taylor and Kate Racker Consider Themselves “Sisters” (Exclusive)

Their Secret Lives of Mormon Wives lore is complicated, but their Circle strategy is simple: lean into “the douchebag.”

A new season of The Circle is here, but it’s not just civilians competing this time around. In the Hulu revival series, celebrities are also part of the competition, including The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives star Layla Taylor—though her fellow competitors don’t know that, since she’s playing as a former Mormon bisexual bro named Doug. She’s not doing it alone, either, as she entered the Circle with her bestie Kate Racker. Who is Kate, you ask? I paid a visit to her and Layla’s apartment in Atlanta to get to know her (and her SLOMW lore) a little better.

As you might recall, Kate was first introduced during SLOMW season 2 as Chase McWhorter’s on-again, off-again girlfriend. She later explained to People that she and Chase met just three weeks after she split from her ex-husband (with whom she shares a son, Rowan), and it was through Chase that she met the MomTok crew, including Layla (though fun fact: Kate already knew Jessi Draper, who was her neighbor growing up, as well as Jacob Neeley, who she used to work with in private equity).

It wasn’t until summer 2025, however, that Layla and Kate really cemented their friendship. “Before she started dating Mason [McWhorter], and I was kind of off with Chase, Layla and I were just single together. So we’d go out, we talk every day,” Kate previously told People. “We were kind of each other’s person. We’d FaceTime 24/7 and just got really, really, really close.”

Now they’re spending 24/7 together in the Circle, where—according to Kate—they’ve only gotten into “one tiny little tiff” so far. “We are sisters at this point, and I consider her my sister,” she says, adding, “I think the thing about me and Layla is we’re both like pretty sensitive, but we both love each other so much, and we’re committed [to winning]. Like, I have goosebumps.”

My big question for them: Why did they decide to step into the Circle as a catfish rather than themselves?

“It’s kind of fun to like step into somebody that you’re not,” Layla says.

Kate agrees, adding, “It’s a new experience. I mean, we’re all about new experiences and trying new things and role play. We love role play. We’ll try anything three times.”

And if you’re wondering whether they have a certain man (or a certain pair of brothers) in mind while playing “Doug,” the answer is...not really. “I feel like he’s kind of a collection of the men that we have encountered in our life or even dated in the past,” explains Layla. “Like, all of them.”

“I think we’re trying to lean into the douchebag,” adds Kate. “Not to say all men are douchebags, but no, there’s some great ones. Or, you know, a few.”

Layla then clarifies, “We’re not men haters. No, not at all. We are men observers. And we’ve observed poor behavior from the men that we do know, and we’re just emulating and dramatizing that.”

I also ask Layla if she’s missing her girlfriend Kelsie Chesser while she’s in the Circle, and she says that—while she says, “For sure, you know, I love her dearly”—the harder part is being away from her kids. “Yeah, we both cry every day,” Layla says. “Cried myself to sleep last night. But Kate is my bestie, and there’s no one I’d rather be trapped in here with.”

Since there are players who have yet to be revealed in the Circle, I need to know: Which of the Mormon Wives would they be most excited to see pop up on their screen?

“I would love to see Jessi,” Kate says. “Obviously, Miranda [Hope]. Miranda would be great. Mayci [Neeley]. Like, any of them.”

Layla grins before adding, “Yeah, I think I can say everyone...but one.”

#6880. 20 Years Later, the Best Affordable-Luxury Gift for Teen Girls Is Still a Longchamp Tote

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

There’s a lot of chatter—and, let’s face it, uncertainty—about what teen girls actually want as gifts. I track shopping trends for a living and also happen to be a mom of a Gen Z daughter, so I know how often they change their minds about what’s cool. Fortunately, my daughter is always happy to give me an honest, up-to-date report.

When she graduated from high school, we wanted to get her something nice. More importantly, we wanted to get her something she really wanted. I have to admit I was surprised when she told us she wanted Longchamp’s Le Pliage tote. That’s right, that tote. The one that was all the rage in the mid-aughts.

“It was around 2006 when my best friends and I gifted each other Longchamp totes for our birthdays,” Deputy Managing Editor Cristina Corvino tells me. “They seemed like gold to our teen selves at the time. Who knew they'd still be coveted teen gifts 20 years later? I feel validated!”

On a practical level, getting my daughter an under-$200 designer bag turned out to be a great idea. The tote is made of water-resistant nylon with a leather handle and flap, and I like that it zips all the way shut. It has a roomy main compartment, and it sits comfortably on her shoulder. To this day, it’s the tote she carries around her college campus and the one she brings with her when she comes home to visit.

But, honestly? When I bought it, I had no idea how popular this specific tote is with teen girls. Several of her friends also have the Le Pliage, and when we dropped her off at college, I couldn’t help but notice its iconic silhouette whenever yet another college kid strolled by with one on their arm.

So if you’re looking for a meaningful, decidedly grown-up gift for your stylish teen—and your wallet has grown tired of chasing one fleeting trend after another—I’m willing to bet they’d be thrilled to get a Longchamp. If they already have the Le Pliage tote (clearly, I wouldn’t be surprised), here are a few more thoughtful, under-$250 Longchamp gifts to consider.

#6877. Prince Harry Shocks Viewers With ‘This Morning’ Appearance—Here’s What He Revealed

Prince Harry has surprised This Morning viewers with an extra-long appearance on the most famous sofa in Britain. The Duke of Sussex appeared alongside regular Friday hosts Dermot O’Leary and Alison Hammond and joined them for a few segments throughout the morning show.

Over the course of his appearance on the ITV chat show, Harry—who was there to discuss his work with the Invictus Games—revealed more insight into his children Lilibet and Archie’s new life in the UK and gave a hint at his upcoming Christmas plans with Meghan and their family.

Here’s everything Prince Harry said throughout his appearance on This Morning.

Following the family’s move back to the UK in September earlier this year, Harry shared that the children, Lilibet and Archie, are finally getting British accents having spent most of their childhood so far in California, adding he was “squeezing the accents out of them.”

He also revealed there was a bit of a “culture shift” for the children when it came to their new schooling as they now have to wear school uniform, something they didn’t have to do while in school in the U.S.

“I think the kids being in school uniforms is a big change for them,” he explained. “But it has certainly helped. The confidence in both of them has gone through the roof since being in a UK school, which is really, really nice to see.”

During his chat with Alison and Dermot, the hosts quizzed him on all things UK vs. USA, and Alison asked him for his thoughts on Robert Pattinson being cast as James Bond, to which Prince Harry responded, “I think it’s in great hands.” He also added he couldn’t wait to see the film.

He was also asked whether he preferred EastEnders or Suits (which is the show his wife Meghan Markle is most known for acting in) and he ultimately picked Suits.

Every Friday on This Morning, hosts Dermot and Alison pick a variety of music to play throughout the show, and Harry was also given the chance to pick a song for Friday.

He revealed his choice was “Runnin’ (Lose It All)” by Naughty Boy feat. Beyoncé and Arrow Benjamin, and added it’s the song he listens to while doing a cold plunge.

The Duke of Sussex joined Dermot and Alison for the cooking segment of the series where they were learning how to create a Christmas cake with Phil Vickery.

During the segment, the hosts asked him about whether he liked to bake and he revealed that children Lilibet and Archie liked to bake, but that the whole family was a fan of The Great British Bake Off, which is of course presented by Alison.

Harry added that the series was good this year and he was a big fan of Tom to win the series.

As the cooking segment wound down, Alison asked him who would be doing the cooking at Christmas in his home this year.

He went on to say his wife and mother-in-law were the better cooks in his household and so they would be doing the cooking for the festive season. The Duke also revealed they may do things differently this year and have a Christmas gumbo, too!

#6876. Noah Wrapped the Converse Chuck 70 in Menswear’s Favorite Tartan

When Converse and Noah collaborated for the first time earlier this year, the most surprising thing was that it’d taken so long for them to link up. The sneaker OGs and New York menswear mavens make as much sense together as peanut butter and jelly—both brands flow effortlessly between worlds, embraced by punks and preps and hoopers and skaters alike. Thankfully, the duo have been making up for lost time, kicking their partnership off with a couple of killer Chuck 70s in the spring before putting out an understated banger of a spin on the Louie Lopez 2 over the summer. Now, they’re capping off their banner year with one last go-round—another jazzed-up Chuck Taylor that’s about as holiday-ready as it gets.

Just in time for turkey season, Noah has wrapped the Converse Chuck 70 Low in one of the most beloved patterns in menswear: Black Watch tartan. The green, navy, and black plaid was originally worn by squadrons patrolling the Scottish Highlands in the 18th century, with its subdued tones meant to help wearers blend into their surroundings. Over the past several decades, it’s been embraced by fashion at large, but particularly Trad Ivy enthusiasts, who tend to sport the striking tartan on everything from sport coats and scarves during the colder months.

In addition to the sharp Black Watch canvas uppers, Noah made a handful of other subtle changes to the classic silhouette, adding its branding to the “license plate” on the shoe’s heel, removing the wing tongue stitch, and swapping in a woven tongue tag. The kicks feature the standard white laces but also come with a mustard yellow pair for an extra boost of color.

If you’re looking to dress festively but aren’t all that into freaky patterned sweaters, the latest Converse x Noah Chuck 70 Low might just be the ticket. The shoes drop tomorrow, November 19, for $112 via both Converse and Noah.

#6873. Why Men Are Posting Thirst Traps in Red Light Face Masks

Gonzalo Fernández Escribano knew all about the benefits of red-light therapy for his skin. Fewer wrinkles, a brighter complexion, minimizing acne—he’d experienced it all firsthand when he tried out LED masks during his facials. In March 2025, he purchased one for at-home use. Little did he know it would come with an extra benefit as a provocative prop for a sexy photo.

"I use my red-light mask right before bed, so I’m already shirtless,” says Escribano. “Let’s just say the sexy-looking picture is easily convenient to take.”

Escribano is one of many men online who’ve embraced the red-light thirst trap. More and more, image-conscious guys with followings built around some combination of beauty, fitness, wellness, travel, are pairing their red-light routines with the occasional shirtless selfie. The trend is especially prevalent in gay-male internet culture, where faceless torsos shots, sometimes featuring beefy chests and six-pack abs, have been part of the visual language of dating apps and social media for years. A shirtless red-light-mask pic is a variation on the theme: The body and the anonymity are still there—only now there’s a skincare routine attached.

Jayson Stacy, a celebrity groomer, noticed his friend wearing an LED in his Instagram profile picture three years ago. At the same time, Stacy had recently gotten a mask himself and decided to follow suit, posting a photo of his own after Mardi Gras under the guise of some much needed recovery.

“It’s almost like a sexy Halloween costume, but you don’t have to wait until October,” Stacy says. Escribano agrees, saying it makes him look like a superhero. Strong shadows, glowing skin, and a Sith-like red glow mix together in a thirsty trifecta meant to slow someone’s scrolling.

For Stacy, red-light masks strike the perfect balance between wellness and plausible deniability. “You’re supposed to use the mask after you wash your face, so most are fresh out of the shower before using,” he says. “It’s like, ‘Oh, look at my morning routine,’ which gives the perfect excuse to be mostly unclothed. Then you have the mask on, you feel like someone can’t see your face, so it can feel like a layer of protection.” In other words, the skin care routine becomes the reason for the thirst trap, even though it’s only half the motivation.

Red-light therapy is a noninvasive treatment that uses low-level LED light to stimulate cellular activity and boost collagen production. The technology has become especially prominent over the last few years thanks to its ability to reduce inflammation, soften fine lines, and promote skin and muscle recovery and hair growth. (That is, as long as you are willing to invest in high-quality equipment and use it every single day).

Celebrities have been documenting their LED routines for years, from January Jones to Kim Kardashian. But those posts were mostly framed around the masks’ intended purpose: improving your skin. Somewhere along the way, though, a certain breed of extremely online men—trainers, models, artists, content creators, and guys who simply know their angles—found a second purpose for the masks: as an accessory for a hot photo. The masks are turning up in bathroom mirror pics, bedroom shots, and buried several slides deep in otherwise unrelated photo dumps, often without any mention of skin care at all. And judging by the comments—fire emojis, heart eyes, and the occasional “yummy”—their followers seem to be noticing something other than the mask.

The thirst traps are also surfacing on social media at a time when curiosity around red-light therapy is booming. A 2025 study analyzing Google Trends data found that search interest across red-light-therapy-related terms increased 118% after February 2024. And by 2025, Google reported that searches for “red light therapy” had reached an all-time high. As these futuristic devices have rapidly flooded our feeds, maybe it was only a matter of time before they made their way out of the skin care selfie and into something a little thirstier.

In some ways these images are being ushered into well-trodden territory. In 2021, TikTok’s viral Silhouette Challenge had users transform themselves into barely distinguishable, body-forward silhouettes under a red filter, turning the color itself into a visual shorthand for sex appeal. Meanwhile, for gay men, shirtless photos are hardly a novelty, especially on dating apps like Grindr and Sniffies, where headless torsos are practically part of the experience. “The headless torsos definitely all blend together at this point,” says John Arthur Hill, who compares swiping through hookup apps to looking into “a lukewarm batch of Chili’s skillet queso” and trying to figure out what’s cheese, meat, or a bell pepper.

And even when the most impressive six-pack can get lost in an endless doom scroll, red light can add just enough intrigue to get someone to look twice. For Adam Pent, an OnlyFans creator who’s no stranger to posting thirst traps himself, that curiosity is part of the appeal. “It [a red-light-mask thirst trap] is definitely more interesting because I’d be curious about who’s behind the mask,” he says. To Pent, there’s something creative about the whole thing. “I guess it makes the photo a bit more artful too? ‘Art thot’ vibes I guess, unless it looks like sponsored content.” It also makes him want to shop. “I’m less focused on the body and more like, ‘Should I just get one already?’”

The red-light thirst trap also holds a certain mysterious appeal. “I’ve been posting ‘masked’ selfies for quite some time because I think it’s a really hot aesthetic and such a cool fantasy,” Michael Anthony, a celebrity makeup artist, says. For him, it’s not just about looking good in the photos. “The fantasy of anonymity and pleasure, while doing something for yourself that will make you look and feel better—it’s all so sexy.”

An LED face mask is a luxurious way to get an at-home glow, and Shark's is one of our favorites. It helps to fight acne and fine lines, and it can help depuff around the eyes for those long nights.

Tattoo artist Alvin Archibald sees a similar appeal. “There’s a subtle tension between vulnerability and control that makes it interesting,” he says. “The mask takes away your facial expressions, so the body becomes the focus. It creates this interesting dynamic where you’re both exposed and concealed at the same time. There’s something almost intimate about it, like you’re getting ready for an audience without necessarily admitting it.”

A senior royal aide says the Prince and Princess of Wales are “very much a team” after their whirlwind India tour.

The Prince and Princess of Wales made headlines this week on their royal visit to India, which saw them attend the 2026 Earthshot Prize Awards ceremony, meet the country’s prime minister, drive a tuk-tuk, and even ride the metro. Now that they’re back in the UK, those in the couple’s inner circle have been reflecting on the trip, and one thing is clear: William and Kate are closer than ever.

Speaking to reporters (via the BBC), the pair’s most senior advisor described them as “very much a team” following their multiday tour in India. “It has been particularly special for them to experience this moment together,” said private secretary Ian Patrick. “They are very much a team.”

Commenting on William and Kate’s trip more broadly, the private secretary added, “The prince and princess have had a thoroughly enjoyable time in India. They’ve been incredibly touched by the warmth of the welcome they’ve received.”

Over the past few days, the Waleses have kept fans up to date with their adventures in India by sharing various photos and video clips on Instagram. In one down-to-earth video, William joined locals for a game of gully cricket in Mumbai. #6868. APC Just Dropped a Pair of Incredibly Clean Casios

We’re in a great era for affordable fashion-collab watches. Timex is leading the way: its link-ups with Noah, Todd Snyder, and J. Crew are masterclasses in adding personality to classic designs without going OTT. But don’t count Casio out of the race. The Japanese giant has just followed up its 2024 collab with APC. The pair are perfectly matched: Casio watches are known for being beautifully functional and understated—adjectives that fit APC’s spartan aesthetic too.

The first collaboration between the two brands saw them rework the hallowed A1000 digital-display watch. That release stripped back a lot of the original’s markings and added the APC logo on the dial. This time around, it's the AQ-240 getting a makeover. The case is squarer than the rectangular A1000, and while there’s a narrow window for a digital display, the watch maintains the traditional analog format with hands and hour markers. Here, you get the best of both worlds. The watch is sold out on APC's website but still available through Casio, so act fast if you want one. The silver variant is $110, and the gold goes for $135.

What about the APC-ness of it all? That part is the opposite of loud: as with the previous collab, there isn’t much giving away the brand's participation. We’ve got the APC logo on the dial and the buckle, plus the brand’s guitar-and-dagger insignia engraved out of sight on the caseback. Casio already offers the regular A1000 in a bunch of different colorways, but these two new ones are made specifically for the APC collab. The release includes a silver case with a dark-green dial and another in gold on gold. Both of the pieces have a brushed sun-ray effect on the dial. The vibe is refined and stripped back; this is a fashion watch that almost feels anti-fashion.

Maybe the best way to think about it is as the reverse side of the coin to the excellent new G-Shock 2500 Series. Both come from Casio, and both are minimal and functional. But the G-Shocks are big and bulky, with a design that seems to tip its hat to Patek Philippe’s Nautilus. These new ones are slim and nearly dressy. Put the two new releases together, and you’ve got pretty much all bases covered. And if those aforementioned Timex collabs felt a bit too classicist for you, then the Casio-APC partnership takes things in a more modern direction. Far from just being a cash grab, the “fashion watch” is now one of the best routes into affordable, on-point horology.

#6866. Lafayette 148’s Emily Smith on 30 Years in Fashion and Finding Her Uniform

In ELLE’s Office Hours series, we ask people in powerful positions about their first jobs, worst jobs, and everything in between. This month, we sat down with Lafayette 148 creative director Emily Smith as the New York-based fashion house celebrates its 30th anniversary. Smith has spent more than two decades at the helm of the brand, working her way up from a part-time freelancer to creative lead. But her fashion ambitions began long before she arrived in New York. Growing up in Atlanta, she ran her own neighborhood art camp before discovering designers like Donna Karan and realizing fashion could be a career. Now, Smith is celebrating the women who have shaped Lafayette 148 through its “Uniform” campaign, which highlights accomplished New Yorkers and their individual approaches to personal style. Below, Smith reflects on her unconventional start, why she’s stayed at the company for over 20 years, and the career advice she wishes she’d followed sooner.

I ran a camp in my neighborhood because, at the time, I thought I wanted to be an art teacher. It was basically babysitting all the neighborhood kids, but I created a flyer, got paid for it, and did the whole thing. I started in second grade, and we did it every year for about eight years. I even started hiring people so we could have two classes going at once. We had swimming lessons. The local news even came and did a segment on it. I loved decorating my classroom and setting everything up. My parents were so nice to give me the garage and basement to do it all. Not necessarily an official job, but my first money-making moment.

I was a bagger at Kroger. It just was not for me.

I was always creative growing up. At a certain point, I discovered Donna Karan. It’s kind of crazy when you see somebody doing something and suddenly realize, Oh, that’s a career. I can do that. I became obsessed with everything in the ’90s, all the New York fashion at the time. That was when I decided I wanted to pursue it. When college applications came around, my mom really encouraged me to go to art school. I started in fashion, and that became my focus. Growing up in Atlanta, I wasn’t necessarily surrounded by a big fashion community. But my dad’s office was across the street from the art museum at one point, and I remember going there and having that exposure. My mom was always crafty and creative, too. She really encouraged us to be who we were.

There’s a language creative people have that’s a little different from the way business people communicate. When I went to art school, I felt like I’d found my people. It wasn’t just fashion people. It was photographers, fine artists, and all these different kinds of creatives. Being around people who thought about things differently was really inspiring. Even today, I thrive around people who have creative insight. There’s always a little more depth to the way they see things.

I interned at Bill Blass for about six months. I moved to New York in July 2001, and we had our fashion show on September 10. I remember Marc Jacobs had his show that day, too, and everybody was celebrating. People were saying, “Just come a little late to work tomorrow.” And then, obviously, September 11 happened. A week later, the entire team was let go, from the top down. What I learned from that experience was the fragility of everything. Don’t take anything for granted. Anything can come and go in a moment. But I also worked with Lars Nilsson and Hervé Pierre Braillard, who were just the kindest people. This industry has a little bit of everything, but I loved that they led with kindness, even when they were under so much pressure. As an intern, I was literally carrying ironing boards down the street and doing a sketch here or there. It was very much about doing whatever needed to be done.

After my internship, I had a brief stint of freelancing. Those were some long months. I remember coming home from one job and my roommates asking how my day was. I said, “Well, I was putting pigs on socks.” I knew I wouldn’t be going back. Freelancing is hard. It’s a grind, but you meet a lot of great people. I actually made some really good contacts during that time. I eventually met Deirdre [Quinn], Lafayette 148’s founder. She met with me on a Saturday, which I thought was incredible. I was really just looking for advice. I wasn’t looking for a job, and I didn’t even know much about the brand. It was only five years old. She was so kind, full of knowledge, and passionate. After I spent a year freelancing and meeting people who seemed so over the industry, her enthusiasm reminded me why I wanted to do this in the first place. She offered me a part-time freelance position, and the rest is history.

In the beginning, security was really important to me. The idea of losing a job and having to pay rent was scary, so that was definitely part of it initially. But really, it was the growth. Within a couple of years, they were sending me to the factory. It was a small, tight-knit company, so I was able to wear a lot of different hats. I had a mentor named Barbara Gast who was traveling back and forth from Italy, and I was learning so much from her. I’d compare notes with friends in the industry and realize how much hands-on experience I was getting. I understood the value of that and really loved being so close to the product. It never got boring. And whenever things did get slightly boring, I’d ask, “Okay, what’s next?” And there was always something. It’s such an entrepreneurial place that you can almost create your next opportunity. That’s what made it so exciting.

I think if you asked Deirdre, she’d have a different answer because she has her own experience of building the company. For me, it’s about having started at a company that was really a factory and being part of the journey of building its identity and what it stands for. I was the kid running up and down the factory floors collecting samples. That hustle and bustle was so innate to who the brand was. To me, it was really exciting to build more of a brand story around that. Thirty years later, we’re still celebrating those roots and making sure we haven’t strayed too far from them, while continuing to evolve. Being part of that journey has been really exciting.

The constant has always been this idea of an accomplished woman. She’s always been at the forefront of who we’re designing for. At the time, it was the career woman. But now, what does that even mean anymore? What does that look like? There are so many different versions of that woman and so many different ways people dress. At the heart of it, she’s a woman who knows what she wants. She’s busy and has a really full life, whether she’s starting her career or ending it. They can all find something in the clothes.

For us, it was about choosing people who inspire us. Number one, they had to be New York women. That was the base. We wanted women who make us want to be better ourselves. From someone like Deborah Berke, the architect, to women who have built incredible careers in other fields, it’s important to shine a light on them. I think back to when I was a kid and how much you need that kind of inspiration. Even at a certain stage in your career, when you’re wondering what the point is, it’s nice to have people who inspire you. These are women who have really built something for themselves, and I think that’s worth celebrating.

Sophie de Rakoff said something during the project that I really liked, and I’m going to steal it: “Dressing with intention.” When you know yourself, you know what works on you. It’s no longer about dressing to impress. There’s a confidence in knowing what works for you and making it work. We’re all so busy. Who has time to overthink getting dressed? You know what works, and you build a system around that. You’re able to create a wardrobe that really works for you. Everybody has a different definition of a uniform, and that was the whole point of the project.

When I first started in the industry, I was a total hippie. I was making my own clothes, and they were terrible. Absolutely falling apart. It was what it was. I think part of the change comes from not having as much time as you did when you were younger. But it’s also about simplicity and comfort. I know that if I’m going to be on the floor pinning something but also have to give a presentation, there are certain pieces that work for both. I can be comfortable and still feel appropriately dressed. I’ve found a handful of things that work for me, and I don’t have to think too much about them anymore. A crisp, oversize white cotton button-down is half my closet. I also love a soft skirt and a big chunky sweater. Those are the pieces I always go back to and interchange depending on the day. I even wear sweaters in the summer. It’s cold in the Navy Yard!

Our pants fit incredibly well. The moment somebody gets into our pants, they’ll love us forever. Then it’s the jacket and the white shirt. Those three things are our gateway pieces, and everything else follows. You need a good blazer and a good leather piece, too. Once you find those foundational pieces that really work for you, everything else becomes easier.

I really want to see more women in our clothes. It’s not just about growing the business. It makes me happy when I see people put on our clothes and feel really good. We work so hard to make sure things fit the way a woman wants them to fit. I just want more people to enjoy that.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

#6861. Are You a Super Ager? This Competition Will Be the Judge of That

Alan Krepack stands with his hands on his hips and takes a deep breath as he sizes up the Over Under: an obstacle with a high wooden box and low wooden beam spread 10 meters apart. Music booms over the speakers and other competitors shuffle around the cavernous room of the Armory Track in Washington Heights, New York, but Krepack stays laser-focused, thanks to years of meditation practice.

As soon as the volunteer says “go” he sprints to the box, plants his hands, and hoists his feet up and over. As he lands on the other side, he sprints to the beam, dropping down into an army crawl. “Take your time, take your time,” his son yells from the sidelines. Krepack reaches the far end, rushes back, and repeats the sequence again before crossing the line in 51 seconds. To cool down, he paces back and forth with his hands on his head. “I think I’m a sprinter, I’m a jumper, but then it’s like, fuck, no, I’m 73 years old,” he says, catching his breath.

The challenge is just the first of eight trials Krepeck will tackle throughout the day alongside his sons, Harrison and Aaron, at the Super Age Games, a fitness and mental competition designed to assess abilities associated with healthy aging. On November 7th, the inaugural day-long event drew 530 athletes from 42 states and 15 countries, and about 70% of participants were between the ages of 40 and 64. Krepeck agreed to let me shadow him to understand this new event through the lens of someone who is, in fact, aging, and would like to do so gracefully.

The name of the Games comes from a buzzword that originated in the longevity world: super ager. The term was originally coined by Northwestern University researchers to describe adults over 80 whose memories rival those of people 30 years younger. These days it’s thrown around on podcasts and in books to describe anyone who defies both the typical trajectory of both cognitive and physical aging.

The inaugural Super Age Games drew 530 athletes from 42 states and 15 countries.

From crawling across a cargo net to reading microexpressions, the challenges in the competition measure longevity markers like functional strength, aerobic capacity, balance, and cognitive skill. At the end, athletes see where they rank among their age group and receive their “Super Age,” learning how their body’s physical fitness stacks up against the general U.S. population’s.

In an era where your average middle-aged midwestern dad is training for Hyrox, and a new, outlandishly extreme challenge is being invented every few months, the Super Age games are a low-key counterpoint to raw, stunty, athleticism, born from the metric-obsessed fitness subcultures who prize longevity above all. And now that fitness trackers like Whoop or Apple Watch can track VO2max, heart rate variability, and resting heart rates, that group continues to grow. Along the way, these stats have become status symbols on par with owning a Lamborghini. And now that overall health is quantifiable, fitness values have become less about just how fast you run or how much you lift, but how slowly aging.

It follows that, despite its name, Super Age Games isn’t just for aging athletes. The crowd there leaned middle-aged, and the youngest person at the competition was 21. As we stood by the dead hang station, Krepak pointed across the room at a brolic young man who looked like he could be an NFL prospect: “That dude’s a serious athlete,” he says, as we watched him run around the track cradling a sandbag on his shoulder.

Unlike competitions like Hyrox, where athletes race through a sequence of workouts against the clock, the Super Age Games allows competitors to rest as long as they need between trials. In fact, Junior Kennedy, the fitness program director for the Games, encourages it. “There's no rush,” he says. “The goal is to get through each event as intentionally as you can. We need to understand what it means to recover.”

Each trial was developed with a certain longevity marker in mind, and developed with input from researchers at the Buck Institute for Research on Aging, a leading nonprofit research center based in Novato, California dedicated to understanding aging. The Over Under, for example, tests agility by judging how fast you can change levels and switch directions. Meanwhile, the Synapse, a challenge where participants track moving balls on a screen and identify them after they've been shuffled around, is meant to measure cognitive processing.

Challenges in the competition measure longevity markers like functional strength, aerobic capacity, balance, and cognitive skill.

Krepack, a psychologist and lifelong athlete from Mill Valley, California, first heard about the event through its founder, David Stewart. Stewart is a media founder behind two niche longevity platforms, AGEIST and Super Age, where he writes about longevity, wellness, and redefining aging (one of his website testimonial’s says he has made aging “cool and aspirational.”). Krepack’s generally a skeptic of the profit-driven modern wellness industry—“I've always had a jaded view of all of that stuff,” he says—but he has followed both Stewart and the Buck Institutes’ work for years.

The competition appealed to Krepack, in part because he’s spent decades cultivating a consistent exercise routine he’s proud of. He played baseball in college and has spent much of his life skiing, cycling, and playing other sports. These days, his schedule revolves around a handful of rituals: bike rides on Mondays and Fridays, a Saturday hike with a friend, gardening, yoga, and Zen meditation most days. When he signed up for the Games in June, he altered his exercise routine, lifted more weights, practiced hanging from a bar, and experimented with the Neurotracker cognitive test online.

Yet all that training didn’t prepare Krepack for the biggest surprise of his day. After scoring well on both the Inferno (the assault bike VO2max test) and Synapse, next came the fourth trial: the “Tightrope,” a balance challenge that requires competitors to walk along a low beam while carrying a water-filled bag, taking as many consecutive steps as they can in two minutes without falling. He had expected to excel, thanks to years of skiing and yoga. Instead, he struggled to keep steady as the water shifted inside the bag. As his foot slipped off the bar, his eyes got wide. “I can’t believe my balance,” he says. “That’s my one disappointment in life now.”

Alan Krepack's sons Harrison and Aaron during the “tightrope” challenge.

Krepack was still hard on himself as we walked over to the dead hang trial. But after beating both his sons’ times, he seemed to get his competitive spirit back. He pushed through the remaining challenge determined to finish strong. And he did: By the end of the Games, he had placed second overall, with scores adjusted for age.

A few days after the competition, I checked in with Krepack to ask him how he’d processed the event. He responded with screenshots of his scores. His highest score came from the cargo net, which he credited to sheer determination rather than any particular finesse: “due to my will and overcoming an unconventional and desperate style!” he texted me, “Plus my sons were great support and focus partners.”

But the Games were also a reality check about aging: “I left there feeling both young and old,” he tells me on a phone call. As he watched his sons rocket through the obstacles, he was forced to confront how much of his athleticism he’d lost. “They just flew over it. They were so fluid, and so fast,” he says. “I’m not 30 or 40 or 50 or 60 anymore.”

Despite all the longevity industry's promises of maintaining youth, Krepack sees little value in denying the inevitable. Aging well, he says, is about “accepting, improving, and doing your best. It’s not a denial of who you are at this point in life.” The goal, he says, is to make the most of what you’re still capable of.

#6857. Pride and Prejudice’s Rufus Sewell Is the Most Dashing Man on Television

It’s possible that Rufus Sewell is having too much fun right now. Between playing morally ambiguous Vice President (and husband to the U.S. Ambassador to the Court of St. James’s) Hal Wyler in Netflix’s frothy, prestige-y series The Diplomat and the beloved but deeply flawed Mr. Bennet in the streamer’s upcoming adaptation of Pride and Prejudice, he’s cornered the market on lovable rascals. He thrives on “exercises in gray” and relishes “when it goes wrong” for a character. “It’s the opposite of what you want in life,” he tells me. “You want drama, you want mistakes.” These roles offer plenty of both.

Wyler, of The Diplomat, is navigating how to work with the increasingly autocratic and bloody-minded President Grace Penn (Allison Janney) thousands of miles away from his wife Kate (Keri Russell), who is starting to exhibit some of his own manipulative behaviors she claims to abhor. Two centuries prior, Mr. Bennet is reckoning not with geopolitical crises and egomaniacal heads of state, but with the challenges his wife faces in marrying off five eligible, if dowry-poor, daughters.

Sewell sat down for a long chat with GQ about the two series, the value of blending high silliness with high stakes in the same scene, and how bodily functions are an under-appreciated key to unlocking a character’s specificity.

Rufus Sewell: The interesting question for me, for the second half of the season, was how much is Hal truly aware of everything that Kate and everyone else are noticing and concerned about with Grace's behavior. How much does he see? How much is he choosing to pretend not to see in order to stay in the room? He's always believed that access is all, but how much do you compromise in order to stay in the room? Where’s the point when you realize you stayed in the room too long without saying anything, and the moment’s passed, and you are just an accomplice? I found it very difficult to play his seeming unctuousness and obsequiousness with Grace this season, and to not be trying to protect my character in the wrong way, because I felt he was failing.

He’s overestimated his ability to effect change with Grace. Hal was prepared to appear to be supporting her, when he believed in his ability to steer her in a better direction, and I think he goes past that point. When I read the final episode script and saw where he ends up walking out, it gave me the courage to really lean into how much he goes wrong, by his own standards earlier in the season. He begins to lose his way in a way that he never has done before. But I embraced it, knowing that the real gift of the part was getting to play him going wrong and then seemingly correcting himself. We actually shot a speech that he made, just to give us a sense of where the scene was headed and for me to know how to play it. They might end up using some of it in the next season, or they could end up completely changing what was written. Hal coming out of the summit is a momentous decision in terms of what that does for the future and I've got no idea what’s next—I can't wait to start filming season five to find out.

I was always shocked when this show was described as a thriller because I always thought it was a kind of romantic sex comedy with extremely high stakes, because what it reminded me of firstly was some of the comedy of divorce screwball films of the 1940s. The characters may be divorced after a very tempestuous relationship, but they’re interlinked in a way that can never be undone. There's a romance to all of that history.

The other thing that it reminded me of was the kind of films that were made in the '70s—French films where halfway through an argument one of the characters would sit down on the toilet, you know what I mean? The people inside these films remind you of your own relationships. Your long-term relationship might not go to the same extremes, it might not be in the same setting, but I think people are people. What I love about their relationship is their shared wonkiness, and the getting into the nitty-gritty of the politics and taking someone down and hatching a plot whilst fighting over pastries and getting crumbs on the bed—all of that is part of their foreplay.

That is what the show is about, as far as I'm concerned. These are people who wear suits that might stand at a lectern and be on TV, but the fact is, how do you scratch an itch? Do you smell? Do you need to go to the toilet? These are the things that the script leans into a lot, and it might put some people off, but I don't care about that. That's the thing that makes it feel real, and it's so refreshing to play.

He'll flirt with anyone. We all know people like this. It's not even necessarily sexual, he just engages with people on that level and it's a natural thing for him.

I would say Hal’s flirtatiousness being a problem is open for debate, because a lot of what we understand of him is via Kate’s descriptions of him and his behavior. I can and often do see it in a different way. Part of the story of seasons three and four is Kate’s realization that a lot of stuff she thought of as his personality was in fact the mantle of the job that he'd taken on, because we see her doing the same kinds of things now that she is an ambassador. They both have a lot of the same qualities. They're both killers in a sense, and it's been very convenient for her and great for the show for her to introduce the public to Hal through her descriptions. And yet, every time it seems like he's changed character, in fact, it is revealing something from a different perspective.

We learned pretty early on that we were going to be doing some very, very long scenes, and we were given the advantage of seeing the scenes very early so we could slowly gestate the performances. We needed that because Windsor Castle in the show was comprised of several different buildings and locations, some of them with oceans between them, so we’d be shooting some parts the next day, and other bits in four months’ time.

Especially with a two-hander, with such long takes, you can go in and out of being relaxed, unrelaxed, free, slightly bound, and actually you get to the point where you stop worrying about it. It's like being on stage, because you end up too tired to be bad all the time and end up inhabiting the moment and things just happen. I love that all of it was a surprise to us.

I think what is most important in this particular iteration of the relationship between Mr. and Mrs. Bennet is that he comes to realize which ways he's failed his daughters. When Mrs. Bennet says to him in this version, it's easy for you to say that I’m silly or frivolous, but you try being a woman at this age with no means—this is the fight of their lives that he is making these jokes about. And yes, of course he's funny and right in certain ways, but he's also guilty of negligence of his family for the sake of an easy life for himself. The reason they have no money is that so much money has been spent on bonnets. Money could have been saved, but in order to have a quiet life, he's ducked out of fights in order to avoid responsibility.

Exactly. It was a gift in this role to have that moment when he feels that, admits that, and gets to be slightly humbled in this idea of himself that he's been cultivating. He didn’t actively or consciously avoid having this revelation, but that’s the effect of staying in his study, and now he’s facing the consequences. To use very modern terms, he has a revelation of what his pattern has been. It made sense!

One could be forgiven for thinking that Mr. and Mrs. Bennet are two people that used to be attracted to each other and now have nothing in common and don't like each other. I think it's born out in the book, though, the idea that there is still a love there, and especially a shared humor between them, and a bit of a spark remains.

I love the fact that we get to get drunk together and stuff like that. Olivia Colman and I worked on The Father together a few years ago with Anthony Hopkins, which was a very different kind of gig, but as is often the case with things that are very heavy and intense, we had a hoot doing it. I loved working with her, and was very excited about the chance to work with her again. I love the scenes between Mr. Bennet and Mrs. Bennet. They were such a treat for us.

#6855. Move Over, Balaclavas: The Snood Is Set to Be Winter's Biggest Fashion Trend

Alongside their cousin, the hooded scarf!

It was just a few years ago that the balaclava was everywhere. Fashion people referred to it as the winter headpiece you absolutely needed. But now, I’ve noticed a new star, and that’s the snood, alongside the hooded scarf. Most people over the years have blended these three headpieces, but I’m here to inform the masses: you’re probably wearing a snood or hooded scarf now, not a balaclava.

Balaclavas are a full-coverage piece of headgear that wraps around the entire head, neck, and face (leaving only the eyes, nose, or mouth exposed), whereas a snood is a continuous circular loop or tube of fabric designed primarily to insulate the neck and cover the top of the head.

Balaclavas tend to be closer to the face, similar in fit to a ski mask, while snoods are loose-fitting and have a sort of cowl design to them. According to the winter sports community, balaclavas are better for skiing and extreme cold, but for most of us, we’re just looking to warm up a little with our winter accessories.

If you’re ready to wear your own snood this winter season, I’ve gathered some stylish inspo below to help build out those mood boards.

This brown snood moment with a light beige trench coat is peak winter outfit. The headpiece pulls the whole cozy vibes together and gives ultimate cool girl.

As a Floridian girlie, shorts in the winter are my kind of speed. Throw on those uber warm tights I see scrolling down my TikTok ‘for you’ page and you’re in business. The hooded scarf just elevates the whole moment too and keeps your head warm, which is ultimately the goal.

Colorful snoods aren’t always top of the list, especially if you’re looking for a headpiece that can blend with all your outfits. But if you’re hoping to bring a bit more fun to your winter style, then grabbing a snood like this one above is the way to do it.

Monochromatic is always in, but getting super close to a full black look and throwing in a light-colored headpiece is my personal winter hack. It brightens up the whole outfit, but doesn’t a neutral color like the one above still keep the look uniform and stylish.

Aiyana Ishmael is the style editor at Cosmopolitan magazine. In her work, Aiyana focuses on the culture of fashion and how it intertwines and shapes the zeitgeist. She is an award-winning journalist from Miami, Florida, and a graduate of the historically Black university, Florida A&M. She is a 2024 Forbes 30 Under 30: Media honoree, and her debut romance novella PASSING GAME is set to release March of 2027 (831 Stories/Simon & Schuster).

#6853. Highly Specific Gifts for the Pickiest Menswear Guy on Your List

Every menswear enthusiast worth his weight in waxed canvas has at least one peculiar fixation. Maybe it’s the precise weight of his selvedge jeans, the flat-felled seams of his wool overshirt, or the exact province in Japan responsible for his bespoke glasses. It doesn’t matter who he is or how you know him—brother, bestie, boss—he’ll probably tell you about it at length, entirely unprompted. (He means well, really.) That discernment is kind of endearing under most circumstances, but it makes buying him the right gift an absolute frickin’ nightmare.

That’s where we come in. Start by taking a deep breath: nobody expects you to secure their lifelong grail in an extemporaneous fit of yuletide cheer. Do, however, listen to what he’s been saying. Look for items with a little character and a lot of idiosyncracy—think hand-finished button-ups, tweed that feels like you snagged it at a Scottish estate sale, or suede so buttery you could baste a ribeye with it. Even conventional upgrades are fair game, as long as the cozy PJs you’re eyeing feel swankier than most people’s going-out clothes. These are presents that should look dead simple until someone (your brother, bestie, boss, etc.) takes a closer glance and starts rhapsodizing about why they’re not.

No idea where to start? You’re in the right place. Every option below comes endowed with built-in bragging rights—and, y’know, the unfortunate ability to inspire at least one TED Talk at Thanksgiving dinner.

Leave it to Kapital to make a beanie this much fun.

Suiting for a dude who wants to wear it, not look like one.

The type of subtle, endlessly-layerable knit that ends up in heavy rotation from November through March.

Primo Scottish cashmere from a mill that's been perfecting it since the 1700s.

Plush enough for staying in, polished enough to wear on a grocery run.

It sounds as good as it looks, which, frankly, is saying something.

Italian style gods have sworn by the Valstarino for nearly a century. Be more like an Italian style god.

The flecks keep it interesting, the merino keeps it itch-free.

Definitive proof that wearing a tie can feel like a choice instead of a begrudging concession to dress code.

If his getaways are getting more far-flung, his weekender should keep up.

Some thoughts deserve better than the Notes app. Smythson's compact notebooks make every to-do list feel like a memoir in progress.

Because when you own a pair of silk pajamas you start hosting brunch just to show them off.

Think of these as chinos that went to finishing school, with a garment-dyed color that only deepens every time he wears 'em.

Loewe's leather puzzle design cardholder is a perfect replacement for the overstuffed wallet the fussy fella in your life has been holding onto.

#6849. How to Grow a Beard

So you want to learn how to grow a beard. First off, you should know that it helps to have the right genetics. Second, you have to stay committed to the endeavor. Too often, beard-growing attempts end before they’ve even had a chance to establish itself.

“I’d say give it at least three to four weeks of solid growth before you even think about touching it with clippers,” Victor Mendoza, co-founder of beard care brand Viking Revolution, says. “Your beard needs time to actually show you what it’s going to look like, and that growth period lets you see the natural direction everything’s growing in. Plus, some hairs take longer to come in than others, so patience is key.”

There’s a sweet spot between letting your facial hair grow out and knowing when it’s time to take the trimmers for the best results possible, even if you can’t go full lumberjack. These are the best tips to maximize your hair follicles’ potential whether this is your first beard or your fiftieth.

There are many different beard styles to choose from; you don’t need to stick to neatly manicured, uniform look. Let your growth be influenced by the style you want to achieve.

“Your genes determine hair thickness, growth rate, density, and color,” Mendoza says. “If your dad or granddad had a thin beard, you’re probably dealing with the same hand, and that’s just life.”

If your beard is patchy, or if it has a unique growth pattern (sparse on the cheeks, full on the goatee, for example), then you should set your expectations properly. This goes for beards big, small, and medium alike. And while nutrition is important for hair growth, you can’t cheat genetics with your diet.

Patience is a virtue. Not only can a little time help fill in some patchiness, but it’s also requisite for almost any style. A beard transforms into something new every week. So, if it’s seeming like you’ll never reach your goal—in particular if it’s a long beard—then you honestly might wake up one morning and realize that you’ve arrived at the destination.

Mendoza says to wait at least two weeks before you start to do a cleanup, but only around the ends, i.e. the hairs that aren’t within the perimeter in which you want your beard to grow. That’ll help you figure out your beard’s natural shape.

One of the tenets of beard maintenance is to keep it defined. In short: shave the neckline and clean up the cheek lines. These keep it more beard-like and less neckbeard-like—unless, for some reason, a wooly stoner beard is your aim. In that case, let it grow wild and free.

The second way to keep things looking intentional is to sculpt and trim your beard as it grows. It might feel counterproductive—and counter to #2—but it’s the same way you need to get haircuts while growing out your hair. As Mendoza says, this helps direct the hair exactly where it needs to be: Fuller in some spots, less so in others. The styling products in the next tip can of course help influence things, but you foremost want to keep hairs in check as needed: This can mean spot-checking strays, snipping away split ends, or simply taking out some bulk. You’ll want a beard trimmer or a sharp pair of scissors.

There are a handful of beard products you should consider picking up if you’re serious about proper care and maintenance. A few drops of beard oil will absorb quickly and give your hair the right nutrients (plus a pinch of shine), while also making it less wild and frizzy. You can also swap in a beard balm or cream, something slightly heftier than an oil, to help style the beard and keep it in place all day. What’s important is that you clean your beard every day with an actual beard wash and not just a regular shampoo, which Mendoza says will dry out your beard and your face.

Weekly maintenance will consist of a deep condition, which you can do in the shower. “If you’re doing those things consistently, your beard is going to look way healthier and fuller than most guys out there,” he says.

Beard brushes help exfoliate the skin beneath the beard, for starters, which lifts dead skin cells and prevents future flaking. Moreover, brushing your beard at the end of each day will ensure its health and tame-ability in the long haul. This distributes the natural oils produced in the skin, which otherwise collect at the base of the shaft and only nourish the bottom of each hair. This oil distribution prevents breakage, frizzing, and split ends. (This is a great time to apply a few drops of beard oil as a booster, especially after a shower.)

Once your base has grown out—this can take months—it’s time to really make it yours. One of our favorites is to snip the bridge between the mustache and the chin, thus marooning the mustache. Or, pare back the beard itself, leaving a fuller mustache. You have lots of options, and it’s your privilege as a beard grower to flaunt it.

#6848. These Evergreen Perfumes Are Perfect for the Holidays (And Won’t Make You Smell Like a Candle)

Turns out there are a million ways to wear pine, balsam, and juniper notes.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

As much as I can appreciate a good gourmand or fruity floral, I am a spicy, woody scent girl through and through. And when the wintertime hits, there’s nothing better than the fresh fragrance of coniferous evergreens: pine, spruce, juniper, cypress, cedar, hemlock... if a plant is spiky and verdant, I want to smell it. Until recently, these notes were something I associated with candles—lovely to burn while I was watching a movie by the fire, not for spritzing onto myself during the day.

But, in the past few years, a veritable sleigh-load of evergreen-forward perfumes have launched, and I’ve deeply enjoyed almost every one I’ve sniffed. So, in the spirit of the holiday season, I’ve chatted with some experts about why this particular subcategory of woody fragrance seems to be so popular now and what notes pair best with evergreen for a wearable scent, plus rounded up a few of our perfumes with notes like pine, fir, cypress, spruce, and juniper that are perfect for this most festive time of year.

This rise in evergreen notes aligns quite well with what’s going on in the rest of the perfume world. “When it comes to perfume, this year has been all about touching grass, getting back to nature and living IRL,” says Steven Gontarski, senior manager of brand and public relations at retailer Luckyscent and host of the It’s Just Perfume podcast. “These scents remind us that we're actually living beings with a relationship to the natural world and not just disembodied fingers scrolling on endless screens.”

While we saw nature-inspired perfumes that emulate the sun, juicy berries, even the stinkiest flower in the world launch this fall, so it make sense that the plants most associated with winter are getting their time in the scent spotlight right now. There’s also something inherently cozy about pine.

“Evergreen is what comfort smells like when it isn’t dessert,” says Jeniece Trizzino, vice president of innovation and physical product at fragrance companies Scentbird and Drift. Sweet gourmands have held on to the “most comforting scent category” for a while, but Trizzino agrees that people are seeking a connection to nature now.

“Old trees, forest floor, damp earth: These scents give people a way to reset,” she says.

Evergreen notes are fairly versatile, though they tend to find their way into scents that lean, for lack of a better word, unisex rather than what we think of as traditionally feminine fragrances.

Gontarski loves notes of evergreen paired with incense, which adds a smokiness to the scent. He’s a fan of bright citrus notes too, saying they create “a perfect counterpoint” to resinous notes like pine.

“The best pairings put evergreen somewhere real,” Trizzino says—in other words, these woodsy notes pair best with other scents you can find in nature. She loves them with herbs like mint, basil, or eucalyptus (a different type of evergreen, BTW) and damp notes like moss, wet stone, or ocean air. If you’re still seeking the warmth that’s so common with winter gourmands, Trizzino says evergreen also pairs well with amber, musk, tobacco, and whiskey notes.

Kicking off this list with what I feel comfortable calling the most-likely-to-please-everyone evergreen scent. Yes, the juniper and cedar make for an undoubtedly pine-y fragrance, but it’s paired with warm, creamy notes of cardamom, suede, bourbon, and vanilla that almost bring it into gourmand territory.

This year, Nest turned its best-selling Holiday candle into a perfume you can wear. I’ll admit, I was skeptical that it would turn out well, but the brand really nailed it: The scent is much juicier than the candle, thanks to grapefruit and cranberry, but still has that familiar balsam note.

If you like a juicier scent story, surprise! There’s even an evergreen fragrance for you. This one goes dual meaning on the name “pine,” with notes of pineapple and pine tree, plus some florals and citrus. The result is something that truly works year-round.

This scent smells way more expensive than its under-$50 price tag. Evergreen really leads the charge too: There’s both fir balsam and torched cedar, paired with patchouli and vanilla for a smoky, warm fragrance.

Come for the gorgeous bottle, stay for the invigorating blend of pine, bergamot, eucalyptus, and cypress. It’s not quite as good as a Mediterranean vacation, but it’ll do until I save up enough miles for another trip.

Both my partner and I love this savory gourmand scent. Coriander mixes with juniper for a bright, peppery opening, but it dries down to a musky, spicy, floral thanks to clove, clary sage, magnolia, and mace.

“My all-time favorite is Santal 33,” Trizzino says. “I like woods that are warm and a little creamy.” You’ve almost definitely smelled Santal 33 before, but you might not have realized it has an evergreen (cedar) at its heart.

This aquatic evergreen scent from trendy fragrance brand Noyz is similar in vibes to Santal 33, but a bit more subtle on the skin. It really does smell like the inside of a spa you’d be thrilled to visit on your shittiest of days.

This is a perfume that made me totally forget I’m not typically a fan of rose scents. D’Orsay pairs the flower with a cypress, vetiver, and patchouli base that all adds up to a fragrance that feels both familiar and brand new—like an acoustic cover of your favorite song from high school.

Another cult classic that’s packed with evergreen notes, Gypsy Water opens with juniper berries and bergamot, has pine needles and incense at its heart, then finishes off with a cozy amber-vanilla-sandalwood base.

Fresh, laundry day-inspired scents are another big trend right now. Indie brand Cayla Gray’s take is (as the name implies) quite floral, but there’s white cedar at the base that makes this fragrance smell like walking in the woods on a crisp winter morning.

Nightchild is one of Gontarski’s favorite examples of an evergreen scent. In it, “incense pairs beautifully with evergreen notes,” he says. “Perfumer Michael Nordstrand tells a story of moonlight penetrating a forest canopy with frankincense mimicking its silver light.”

Orphéon is a classic spicy evergreen scent that’s beloved by many already. Named for a Parisian jazz bar that was popular in the 1960s, the vibe is sexy, smoky, and a little bit mysterious. The note of ginger gives it a sweet-spicy touch that you won’t find in any of the other fragrances on this list.

This is a (literally) fresh take on evergreen, perfect for someone who’s seeking a scent that’s light and airy rather than dark and spicy. It’s packed full of familiar herbs; fir, balsam and cypress; plus fig and olive for a touch of savory gourmand goodness.

If you’re looking for a scent that combines both sea and forest, this unisex scent from Giardini di Toscana will suit you just fine. It’s a marine fragrance that blends a little bit of citrus, a little bit of cyprus, and some salty notes for a fragrance that smells like the color turquoise.

Another of Gontarski’s favorites, G.494 has lots of citrus. “Notes of bitter orange and mandarin create a perfect counterpoint to the balsamic resins and evergreen needles,” he says. “It’s clean and invigorating in an understated way.”
